        <!--Definitions-->
        <div class="w3-container w3-padding-64 white" id="definitions">
            <h2>Definitions</h2>

            <p>
                A <b>Computer program</b> is a set of instructions given to a computer to perform specific tasks.
                <br><br>
                A <b>Computer programmer</b> also known as a <b>coder</b> is a person who writes computer programs.
                <br><br>
                A <b>statement in Python</b> is an <b>individual instruction of a program.</b>
                <br><br>
                A <b>Variable</b> is a <b>portion of computer memory</b> for <b>storing a data value.</b>
                <br><br>
                <b>Identifiers</b> are <b>unique names used to identify variables.</b>
                <br><br>
                A <b>string</b> is a variable in Python that contains a <b>collection of characters enclosed in double quotation marks.</b>
                <br><br>
                <b>Object-oriented Programming (OOP) Language</b> is a programming language that uses objects
                to accomplish the goals of a computer program.
                <br><br>
                An <b>Object</b> is anything that can be touched, seen, or used. <br>
                These include: data types (it can be used), variables (it can be used),
                toolbox controls (it can be seen and used) among others. <br><br>
                An <b>Object</b> is also defined an instance of the class (when you use the class that has been created).
                <br><br>
                <b>Instantiation</b> is the act of creating an object.
                <br><br>
                The <b>Behavior</b> of an object includes methods and events.
                <br><br>

            </p>
        </div>
        <!-- End Definitions -->

                    <p>
                        <i>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Please look at the code above. <br>
                            Do you understand that code? <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> The bunch of zeros and ones? <br>
                            I have no idea what those mean.
                        </i> 
                    </p>
                    <p>
                        <code>
                            int num1, num2, sumNum; <br>
                            sumNum = num1 + num2; <br>
                            Console.WriteLine(sumNum);
                        </code>
                    </p>
                    <p>
                        <i>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> What about this code? <br>
                            Do you understand it? <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> I do not fully understand it. <br>
                            However, it makes much sense than the first code? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Both codes execute the same tasks. <br>
                            Ask the user to input those numbers. Add those numbers. Output the sum. <br>
                            Which code is better? <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> The second code of course. <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> The first code is the machine code. <br>
                            It is the machine language. <br>
                            It is a low level language. <br>
                            It is the language the computer understands directly without translation. <br>
                            The second code is the high level language. <br>
                            It is the language humans can read and understand better. <br>
                            Hence, there is the need to <b>compile</b> or <b>interpret</b> the high level language to
                            the machine language
                            so that computers would do what we want them to do.
                        </i>
                    </p>
                    <p>
                        Python is one of the high level languages that we can use. <br>
                        It is a <b>compiled language</b>. <br>
                        This means that it is translated into machine language that can be understood directly by the
                        system. <br>
                        In that regard, a set of tools are needed. <br>
                        The set of tools is known as <b>development toolchain</b> <br>
                        The core of the development toolchain are a <b>compiler</b> and its <b>linker.</b> <br>
                        The best way to compile and run C# programs is to use an Integrated Development Environment
                        (IDE). <br>
                        An IDE typically has several development tools including a text editor and tool to compile C#
                        programs directly. <br>
                        For this course, the Microsoft Visual Studio Community Edition (it is free) is highly
                        recommended. <br>
                        However, you may choose to use any IDE that you wish. <br><br>
                        Here are some <b>free</b> IDEs for Python: <br>
                        (1.) <a href="https://code.visualstudio.com/" target="_blank">Visual Studio Code (Windows)</a> (Highly recommended. The IDE used for this course.) <br>
                        (2.)
                        
                        
                    </p>

                    <h3>Check Your Understanding</h3>
                    <p>
                        (1.) Develop a <b>desktop application</b> titled: <b>Introduction</b> <br>
                        The application should include three buttons: the <b>About</b> button, the <b>Clear</b> button, and the 
                        <b>Exit</b> button. <br>
                        When a user opens the application, it should display a blank page and the three buttons. <br>
                        When the user clicks the <b>About</b> button, these information should be displayed: <br>
                        (a.) Your picture <br>
                        (b.) A brief summary of you. Write at least three sentences about yourself. <br>
                        (c.) One sentence about the course and your major/program. <br>
                        (d.) One sentence about the course expectations/accomplishments. What is your expectation about the course? What 
                        do you intend to accomplish at the end of the course? <br>
                        (e.) Any other thing you would like us to know about you. <br><br>
                        When a user clicks the <b>Clear</b> button, the page should be blank. <br>
                        When a user clicks the <b>Exit</b> button, the application should close. <br><br>
                        <i>
                            I am more interested in the functionality. <br>
                            However, I am also interested in the design/graphics/aesthetics. <br>
                            Make a good design. 
                        </i> <br><br>

                        (2.) Write two different sentences on two different lines. <br>
                        Insert a blank line between the first line and the second line. <br>
                        Do NOT use the escape sequence. <br><br>

                    <p>
                        (1.) Visit my website on <a href="https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/numbers/numbers.html" target="_blank">Numbers</a> <br>
                        (https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/numbers/numbers.html) <br>
                        Choose any applicable question and write a C# program to solve it. <br>
                        Please make sure that the output of your program gives the same result as the solution of the
                        question on my website. <br>
                        You may use Arithmetic Operators, Math Functions, or both. <br><br>

                        (2.) Visit my website on <a href="https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/fractions-decimals-percents/percentApplications.html" target="_blank">Percent Applications</a> <br>
                        (https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/fractions-decimals-percents/percentApplications.html) <br>
                        Choose any applicable question and write a C# program to solve it. <br>
                        Please make sure that the output of your program gives the same result as the solution of the
                        question on my website. <br>
                        You may use Arithmetic Operators, Math Functions, or both. <br><br>
        
                        (3.) <b>Program to determine your age</b> <br>
                        ***Because your age is a PII (Personal Identifiable Information), you may use a fictitious age.
                        However, you need to test it (privately) with your actual age to make sure it works.*** <br>
                        Develop a C# program to determine your age based on these instructions. <br>
                        ONLY one variable is required. <br>
                        <i>
                            (Hint: If only one variable is required, this implies that you need to use Compound
                            Assignment operators)
                        </i> <br>
                        The user wants you to note that these instructions would only work for ages determined this
                        year, and wants you
                        to specify it in the program. <br>
                        (a.) Pick an integer between $0$ and $10$ ($0$ and $10$ exclusive). <br>
                        (b.) Multiply the integer by two. <br>
                        (c.) Add five to the result. <br>
                        (d.) Multiply the result by fifty. <br>
                        (e.) If you already had your birthday this year, add the result to one thousand, seven hundred
                        and seventy-two. 
                        If you have not had your birthday this year, add the result to one thousand, seven hundred
                        and seventy-one. (<i>valid for 2022, adjust accordingly after 2022</i>) <br>
                        (f.) Subtract your four-digit birth year from the result. <br>
                        Run the code and display the output. <br>
                        Explain your result. <br><br>

                        (4.) A client wants you to develop a program to calculate the age of his child. <br>
                        The instructions for the previous question still applies to this question. <br>
                        However, in this case; the program will give the instructions to the child, and the child will
                        respond. <br>
                        This program is an Input/Output program. <br>
                        Develop a C# program for this child. <br><br>

                        (5.) <b>Program to determine your date of birth.</b> <br>
                        ***Because your date of birth is a PII (Personal Identifiable Information), you may use a
                        fictitious age. However, you need to test it (privately) with your actual age to make sure it
                        works.
                        Develop a C# program to determine your date of birth based on these instructions.*** <br>
                        ONLY one variable is required. <br>
                        <i>
                            (Hint: If only one variable is required, this implies that you need to use Compound
                            Assignment operators).
                        </i> <br>
                        (a.) Pick the integer that is your month of birth. <br>
                        (b.) Multiply the integer by two hundred. <br>
                        (c.) Add three hundred and eighty two to the result. <br>
                        (d.) Divide the result by two. <br>
                        (e.) Add your day of birth to the result. <br>
                        (f.) Multiply the result by 100. <br>
                        (g.) Add thirteen thousand, eight hundred and forty to the result. <br>
                        (h.) Add the last two digits of your year of birth to the result. <br>
                        (i.) Subtract thirty two thousand, nine hundred and forty from the result. <br>
                        Run the code and display the output. <br>
                        Explain your result. <br><br>

                        (6.) A client wants you to develop a program to calculate the date of birth of her child. <br>
                        The instruction for the previous question still applies to this question. <br>
                        However, in this case; the program will give the instructions to the child, and the child will
                        respond. <br>
                        This program is an Input/Output program. <br>
                        Develop a C# program for this child. <br><br>

                        (7.) Develop a Body Mass Index (BMI) desktop application using the United States System. <br>
                        Test each condition just like we did in our example for the United States System. <br>
                        Address user-input errors. <br><br>

                        (8.) Develop a Body Mass Index (BMI) console application using the International System. <br>
                        The formula is on the screenshot. <br>
                        Test each condition just like we did in our example for the United States System. <br>
                        Address user-input errors. <br><br>
                        
                        (9.) Develop a Body Mass Index (BMI) desktop application using the International System. <br>
                        The formula is on the screenshot. <br>
                        Test each condition just like we did for the United States System. <br>
                        Address user-input errors. <br><br>

                        Questions (10.) and (11.) are meant to prepare you for the Midterm Project. <br>
                        For Questions (10.) and (11.) <br>
                        Develop a C# program that meets these requirements. <br>
                        (I.) Consider any concept/scenario that uses <b>conditional statements.</b> <br>
                        It will be great if you find a real-world application. However, you can make up your own. <br>
                        If you decide to make up your own, please explain the scenario and the conditions well. <br>
                        If you use a real-world application, please cite your source(s) accordingly. Explain the scenario and the conditions well. <br>
                        Use <b>at least two (two or more)</b> different conditions. <br>
                        The <b>BMI Calculator</b> has <b>four different conditions</b>. <br>     
                        But, please use any application that has at least two different conditions. <br>
                        (II.) Accept user input(s). <br>
                        (III.) Address user input errors. <br>
                        (IV.) Test your program and display the result/output for each condition. <br>
                        (V.) Test your program for user-input errors. <br>
                        (VI.) Submit all the screenshots accordingly. <br><br>

                        (10.) Develop a console application for your concept. <br><br>

                            <b>Student:</b> What is a delimiter? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> A delimiter is a sequence of chararcters that specifies limits/boundaries of
                            characters in a text, expression, word, etc. <br>
                            A good example is the forward slash that you can use to separate that DOB in the form of: mm/dd/yyyy. <br>
                            The forward slash is a delimiter. <br>
                            Another example is the pipe delimiter: mm | dd | yyyy. <br>
                            Another example is the hyphen delimiter: mm-dd-yyyy. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Okay, how do I display the result in that format?
                        </i> <br><br>

                        We are yet to do <b>Statements</b> (in the next Module), but you can give this question a try. <br>
                        A <b>string palindrome</b> is a <b>word</b>, <b>phrase</b>, <b>clause</b> or <b>sequence of characters</b> that is 
                        <b>spelled and read the same way backward and forward</b>. <br>
                        In other words, when one reads it <i>as is</i>, reverses it and reads it again; the pronunciation is the same. <br>
                        Examples include the: <br>
                        Words: <b>HANNAH</b>, <b>BOB</b>, <b>madam</b>, <b>level</b>, <b>civic</b>, <b>racecar</b>, <b>rotor</b>  <br>
                        Phrase: <b>never odd or even</b> <br>
                        Clause: <b>nurses run</b> <br>
                        Sentence: <b>Was it a car or a cat I saw?</b> <br><br>
                        (1.) Write a Python program that asks for user input. <br>
                        Determine if the user input is a string. <br>

                        Inform the user whether it a string palindrome or not. <br>
                        Test at least one example of: Words, Phrase, Clause, and Sentence that was listed. <br>
                        This implies that you should test at least four examples. <br><br>

                <div id="Learn5" class="w3-container menu5 w3-padding white">
                    <h3>Vocabulary Words</h3>
                    <p>
                        statement, control flow tools, flow control, flow of execution, statement block, 
                        nested blocks, <i>if</i> statement, multiple <i>if</i>-s statement, <i>if-elif</i> statement, 
                        <i>for</i> statement, <i>range</i> function, <i>break</i> statement, <i>continue</i> statement, 
                        <i>match</i> statements, control flow tools, declaration statements, expression,
                        expression statements, control flow statements, <br> 
                        selection statements/conditional statements/decision-making statements: if, if-elif, 
                        if-elif-else, multiple ifs, switch, match; <br> 
                        iteration statements/looping statements/loops: for, for-in (range-based loop); <br>
                        jump statements: break, continue; <br>
                        exception handling statements: try, try...except, raise; <br>
                        <i>with</i> statement, <i>pass</i> statement, 
                    </p>
        
                    <h3>Objectives</h3>
                    <p>
                        Students will: <br><br>
                        (1.) Discuss Statements in Python programming language. <br>
                        (2.) Write selection statements. <br>
                        (3.) Write iteration statements. <br>
                        (4.) Write jump statements. <br>
                        (5.) Write some exception handling statements. <br>
                        (6.) Write programs that solve real-world problems using statements. <br>
                        (7.) Complete the Midterm Project on Piecewise Functions. 
                    </p>

                    <h3>Required Readings</h3>

                    <h3>Statements</h3>
                    <p>
                        A <b>statement</b> is an <b>individual instruction of a program.</b> <br>
                        It forms a complete unit of execution. <br>
                        It is equivalent to a sentence in a language. <br>
                        It can consist of a single line of code (single-line statement). <br>
                        It can also consist of multiple lines of code (multi-line statements) in a block, 
                        known as a <b>statement block.</b> <br>
                        A statement block is enclosed in curly braces {} and can contain nested blocks. <br>
                        A <b>block</b> is a group of zero or more statements between balanced curly braces that can be used where a single statement 
                        is allowed. <br>
                        In Example 3 (<b>If-else</b> statement) as seen in the screenshot below; the <b>if</b> statement is the 
                        first block of code and the <b>else</b> statement is the second block of code. <br>
                        The <b>order in which statements are executed in a program</b> is called the 
                        <b>control flow</b> or <b>flow of control</b> otherwise known as <b>flow of execution.</b> <br>
                        The flow of control varies every time a program is run. <br>
                        It depends on how the program reacts to the instruction it receives at run-time. <br><br>
                        There are several types of statements in Python. <br>
                        We have discussed some of them in previous modules. <br>
                        We shall discuss some of them in this module. <br>
                        We shall discuss some of them in subsequent modules. <br>
                        As at today: 03/06/2023; the statements in Python are: <br><br>

                        (1.) <b>Declaration Statement</b> <br>
                        A declaration statement declares a variable or constant. <br>
                        To declare a variable, the data type must be specified as well. You have the option of assigning a value to it. <br>
                        To declare a constant, the keyword must be specified. A value must be assigned to it. <br>
                        <code>
                            // Declaration statement <br>
                            // We started using this statement beginning from Module 2 <br>
                            int num1 = 7; <br>
                        </code> <br><br>

                        (2.) <b>Expression Statement</b> or <b>Expression</b> <br>
                        An expression statement or an expression is a construct made up of variables, operators, and method invocations, 
                        which are constructed according to the syntax of the language, that evaluates to a single value. <br>
                        Expression statements calculate a value must store the value in a variable. <br>
                        <code>
                            // Expression statement<br>
                            // We covered this statement in Module 3 <br>
                            double result = 7 * (5 - 2); <br>
                        </code> <br><br>

                        <b>Control Flow Statements</b> <br>
                        <b>Generally, statements in C# are executed from top to bottom, in the order that they appear.</b> <br>
                        However, control flow statements control the order in which statements are executed. <br>
                        In other words, they break up the flow of the execution of statements by using decision making, looping, and 
                        branching; thereby allowing a program to execute particular blocks of code conditionally. <br>
                        They include: <br><br>

                        (3.) <b>Selection Statements</b> or <b>Conditional statements</b> or <b>Decison-making Statements</b> or <b>Decisions</b> <br>
                        A selection statement allows the user to branch to different code sections depending on one or more conditions. <br>
                        They are the: <b>if</b> statement, <b>if-elif</b> statement, <b>if-elif-else</b> statement, 
                        <b>Multiple ifs</b> statement, and the <b>switch</b> statement. 
                        <br><br>

                        (4.) <b>Iteration Statements</b> or <b>Looping Statements</b> or <b>Loops</b> <br>
                        An iteration statement allows the user to perform the same set of statements repeatedly until a specified condition is met. <br> 
                        It also allows the user to loop through collections such as arrays. <br>
                        They are the: <b>for</b> loop, <b>foreach</b> loop, <b>while</b> loop, and the <b>do-while</b> loop statements. <br><br>                        
                        
                        (5.) <b>Jump Statements</b> or <b>Branching Statements</b> or <b>Branches</b> <br>
                        A jump statement allows the user to transfer control to another section of the code. <br>
                        They are the: <b>break</b> statement, <b>continue</b> statement, <b>goto</b> statement, <b>return</b> statement, 
                        and the <b>default</b> statement. <br><br>

                        Let us discuss the Selection, Iteration, and Jump statements. <br>
                        Then, we can discuss more statements. 
                    </p>

                    <h4>Selection Statements</h4>
                    <p>
                        (1.) <b>If</b> statement <br>
                        This statement is used when there is <b>only one condition.</b> <br>
                        If <i>some condition</i> is met, execute a <i>given task.</i> <br>
                        The <b>If</b> statement is written as:
                    <pre>
                            if (condition) 
                            { 
                                statement;
                            }
                    </pre>
                    C# ignores whitespaces so you can also write it this way: <br>
                    <pre style="text-align: center;">if (condition) { statement; }</pre>
                    <br><br>

                    (2.) <b>If-else</b> statement <br>
                    This statement is used when there are <b>only two conditions.</b> <br>
                    If <i>first condition</i> is met, execute a <i>given task.</i> <br>
                    else execute <i>another task.</i> <br>
                    The <b>else</b> refers to the other condition. <br>
                    Please note that we do not specify the condition for the <b>else</b> statement because we already know it is the other 
                    (remaining) condition. <br>
                    The <b>If-else</b> statement is written as:
                    <pre>
                        if (condition) 
                        {
                            statement;
                        } 
                        else 
                        {
                            statement;
                        } 
                        </pre>
                    C# ignores whitespaces so you can also write it this way: <br>
                    <pre style="text-align: center;">if (condition) { statement; } else { statement; }</pre>
                    We can also write simple <b>If-else</b> statement using the <b>Ternary operator</b> <br>
                    It can be used for simple if-else statements. <br>
                    The syntax is: 
                    <pre>
                        variable = if (condition) ? statement if true : statement if false;
                    </pre>
                    
                    (3.) <b>If-else if-else</b> statement <br>
                    This statement is used when there are 
                    <b>
                        more than two conditions in which the first condition is used for the <i>If</i> statement, other conditions are used for the 
                        <i>else if</i> statements, and the last condition is used for the <i>else</i> statement.
                    </b> <br>
                    Please note that the last condition for the <b>else</b> statement is not specifid in the code because it is the remaining 
                    condition. <br>
                    It is generally known as the remaining condition after all other conditions are specified. <br>
                    Assume we have only four conditions for a particular program: <br>
                    The <b>If-else if-else</b> statement is written as:
                    <pre>
                        if (condition 1) {
                            statement;
                        } else if (condition 2){
                            statement;
                        } else if (condition 3) {
                            statement;
                        } else {
                            statement;
                        } 
                    </pre>
                    Whitespaces are ignored in C#. Therefore you can also write it this way: <br>
                    <pre>
                        if (condition 1) { statement; } 
                        else if (condition 2) { statement; } 
                        else if (condition 3) { statement; } 
                        else {statement; }
                    </pre>
                    Keep in mind that we do not specify condition 4 in the code for the <b>else</b> statement because we
                    already know that the <b>else</b> statement would only be executed if all the other prior conditions are 
                    not met. <br><br>

                    (4.) <b>Multiple Ifs</b> statement <br>
                    This statement is used when there are <b>more than two conditions in which each condition is uniquely different.</b> <br>
                    You can also use this statement if you are not comfortable using the <b>If-else if - else</b>
                    statement. <br>
                    Both statements accomplish the same task. <br>
                    Assume we have only four conditions for a particular program: <br>
                    The <b>Multiple Ifs</b> statement is written as:
                    <pre>
                            if (condition 1) {
                                statement;
                            }
                            if (condition 2){
                                statement;
                            }
                            if (condition 3) {
                                statement;
                            }
                            if (condition 4) {
                                statement;
                            } 
                    </pre>
                    Whitespaces are ignored in C#. Therefore you can also write it this way: <br>
                    <pre>
                            if (condition 1) { statement; } 
                            if (condition 2) { statement; } 
                            if (condition 3) { statement; } 
                            if (condition 4) { statement; }
                    </pre>

                        Review the <a href="https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/numbers/#definitions" target="_blank">Definitions of Numbers</a> <br>
                        (https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/numbers/#definitions) <br>

                    <h4>Piecewise Functions</h4>

                        Please review the 
                        <a href="https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/piecewise-functions/#story" target="_blank">Story on Piecewise Functions</a><br>

                        Let us address user-input errors for the number of burritos that may be out of range in the Piecewise Function. <br>
                        It is important to address user-input errors in any program you write. <br>
                        In this case (for the Story on Piecewise Functions): the program will not display any output if the user enters a negative 
                        number, a zero, or any positive number greater than the upper bound of the last piece. <br>
                        The user may then be confused if he/she does not see any output. <br>
                        We want to avoid that. <br>
                        In the case of user-input error, we want the program to display a message that the user entered something out of range, and 
                        then exit without interrupting the system. <br>
                        Hence, we wrote the message for the user and included the <b>Environment.Exit(0);</b> method to exit the program. <br><br>

                            We did not need to specify the range of numbers that are out of bounds as we did for the Multiple-Ifs statements. <br>
                            In other words, we do not specify the condition for the else statement. <br>

                            Typically, the user is supposed to notice it after the message we wrote for the Multiple-Ifs <br>
                            However, it is better to show the user what was entered, as well as the message. 
                        </i> <br><br>

                        (5.) <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/language-reference/keywords/switch" target="_blank"><b>Switch</b> statement</a> <br>
                        (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/language-reference/keywords/switch) <br>
                        Another selection statement is the switch statement. It is an alternative to an if-else statement if a single expression 
                        is tested against three or more conditions. <br>
                        The <b>switch statement</b> is a selection statement that selects a <b>single switch section</b> to 
                        <b>execute from a list of cases</b> based on a <b>pattern match with the match expression.</b> <br>
                        It includes one or more switch sections. However, it chooses only one section. <br>
                        Each switch section contains one or more case labels followed by one or more statements. <br>
                        The case labels consists of a case and/or the default label. <br>
                        At most one default label may be placed in any swtich section of a switch statement. <br><br>

                        The match expression in a switch statement can be any non-null expression. <br>
                        It is a variable of these data types: character; string; bool; integral values such as integer, long; and enumeration types. <br>  
                        No two case labels may contain the same expression (variable). <br><br>

                        C# does not allow the execution of statements from one switch section to the next switch section. <br>
                        In that regard, each switch section must be exited by using a jump statement such as the <b>break</b>, <b>goto</b>, or 
                        <b>return</b> statement. The <b>break</b> statement is normally used. <br><br>
                
                        Which selection statement should you use? If-else or Switch statement? <br>
                        The option to use if-then-else statements or a switch statement depends on the <b>readability</b> and 
                        <b>the expression that the statement is testing.</b> <br>
                        An if-then-else statement can test expressions based on ranges of values or conditions, while a switch statement tests 
                        expressions based only on a single integer, enumerated value, or String object. <br><br>

                        The general syntax of the <b>switch</b> statement for the Integer data types (Byte, Short, and Integer data types) is:
                        <pre>
                            variable 
                            switch (variable)
                            {
                            case 1: 
                            	statement;
                            	break;
                            case 2:
                            	statement;
                            	break;
                            case 3:
                            	statement;
                            	break;
                            default:
                            	statement;
                            }
                        </pre>

                        Case 1 <br>
                        The <b>switch</b> block compares the value of the variable with 1 <br>
                        If the value of the variable is 1, then it executes the statement in case 1 and breaks, thus ending the program. <br>
                        If the value of the variable is not 1, it moves on to case 2 and compares the value of the variable with 2 <br>
                        If the value of the variable is 2, it executes the statement in case 2, breaks, and ends the program. <br>
                        Otherwise, it moves to case 3. <br>
                        It compares the value of the variable with 3 <br>
                        If that value is 3, it executes the statement in case 3 and breaks. The program ends. <br>
                        If that value is not 3, it executes the statement in the <b>default</b> <br>
                        As noted, the <b>break</b> statement ends the program when the value of the variable is equal to the
                        case number, otherwise the <b>switch</b> statement moves on to the next case and continues the comparison.
                        <br><br>
                        
                        <i>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Have you noticed these Call Center greetings? <br>
                            For English, press 1; Para Español, marque dos. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Yes I have. <br>
                            You mean to say: For Spanish, press 2. <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Yes. <br>
                            Let us write a simple application for that greeting using a simple Select-Case statement. <br>
                            We can add more simple features..."spice it up". 
                        </i> <br><br>

                        Switch Statements: Example 1: Call Center Greetings <br>
                        <img src="images/Module5CSharp/switch-statements-1-1.png" alt="Switch Statements: Example 1-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/Module5CSharp/switch-statements-1-2.png" alt="Switch Statements: Example 1-2"> <br>

                    <h4>Nested Conditional Statements</h4>
                    <p>
                        <b>Nested If-Else Statements</b> <br>
                        We can write more challenging programs using Nested Selection Statements <br>
                        We can use Nested If-Else statements <br><br>

                        One of the syntax for Nested If-Else statement is: 
                        <pre>
                           dataType variable;
                            if condition 1
                            {
                                If condition 2
                                { 
                                    statement;
                                }
                                else
                                { 
                                    statement;
                                }
                            } 
                                 
                            else if condition 3
                            { 
                                if condition 4
                                {
                                    statement;
                                } 
                                else
                                { 
                                    statement;
                                }
                            } 
                            
                            else
                            { 
                                statement; 
                            } 
                        </pre>

                        Notice the order in which each nested conditional statement is closed. <br><br>

                        <b>Would you like to challenge yourself with nested conditional statements?</b> <br>
                        (<i>Just for the fun. Not for a grade</i>) <br>
                        Develop C# programs that uses nested conditional statements to solve these applications. <br><br>

                        (1.) Who qualifies for the $\$1,400$ payment under the American Rescue Plan also known as President Biden's stimulus bill? <br>
                        <a href="https://money.yahoo.com/heres-who-gets-a-1400-payment-under-the-new-stimulus-deal-194708150.html" target="_blank">Third Round of Stimulus Checks: American Rescue Plan</a> <br>
                        (https://money.yahoo.com/heres-who-gets-a-1400-payment-under-the-new-stimulus-deal-194708150.html) <br><br>

                        (I.) Who likes to read? <br>
                        (II.) Who likes bite-sized information? Who likes flowcharts/diagrams to represent information? <br>
                        (III.) Who prefers to just use an application (app) and enter a few details to see if he/she qualifies? <br><br>

                        Who would develop this application: (console, desktop, web, and mobile apps) by using (I.), (II.), and (III.)...the Programmer. <br>
                        Develop a desktop application that addresses all those conditions, and informs any user if he/she qualifies to get the stimulus
                        payment. <br>
                        <i>Notice how important you are</i> &#128522; <br><br>

                        (2.) Advising is not an easy work in tertiary institutions. <br>
                        Placing students to take the correct courses for their majors is an important task. <br>
                        You have to check the student's High School GPA, ACT/SAT test scores, tranfer credits/courses and grades if any, graduation date and previous 
                        courses, grades made in those courses, and the dates those courses were taken, prior experience in field as applicable, and
                        degree in a related field as applicable among others. <br>
                        Without an efficient/effective program to check all these conditions, it is possible for students to take courses that are 
                        irrelevant to their major/program. <br>
                        Imagine if those students paid for those courses. That is similar to spending money on what you don't need. <br>
                        The work of a programmer is to solve problems. <br>

                        Develop a desktop application for advising students in that major. <br>
                        <i>Notice how important you are</i> &#128522; 
                    </p>

                    <h4>Iteration Statements</h4>
                    <p>
                        <b>Iteration Statements</b> repeat a group of statements a specified number of times. <br><br>

                        They are the: <br>
                        (1.) For Loop <br>
                        (2.) While Loop <br>
                        (3.) Do-While Loop <br>
                        (4.) Foreach Loop <br><br>

                        (1.) <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/language-reference/keywords/for" target="_blank"><b>For Loop</b></a> statement <br>
                        (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/language-reference/keywords/for) <br>
                        The <b>for</b> iteration statement executes a statement or a block of statements while a specified Boolean expression evaluates to true. <br>
                        The general syntax of a <b>for loop</b> is:
                        <pre>
                            for (initializer; condition; iterator)
                            {
                        	    statement;
                            }
                        </pre>

                        This is also written as:
                        <pre>
                            for (variable = beginValue; condition; variable compound_assignment someValue)
                            {
                        	    statement;
                            }
                        </pre>

                        where the: <br>
                        <code>for</code> <i>keyword</i> begins the For loop. It is <b>required.</b> <br>
                        <code>initializer</code> is the beginning value of the variable. It can be <b>optional.</b> <br>
                        <code>condition</code> is the condition required for the variable to reach the final value. It can be <b>optional.</b> <br>
                        <code>iterator</code> is the incremental or decremental value for the beginning value of the variable to reach the final value. 
                        It can be <b>optional.</b> <br><br>
                        Declare a variable and <i>initialize</i> it. <br>
                        <i>For</i> the variable to satisfy the <i>condition</i> until the final value of the variable, execute the statement. <br><br>

                        (2.) <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/language-reference/keywords/while" target="_blank"><b>While Loop</b></a> statement <br>
                        (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/language-reference/keywords/while) <br>
                        The <b>while</b> iteration statement executes a statement or a block of statements while a specified Boolean expression 
                        evaluates to true. <br>
                        It executes zero or more times because that Boolean expression is evaluated before each execution of the loop. <br>
                        The general syntax of a <b>while loop</b> is:
                        <pre>
                            initializer;
                            while (condition)
                            {
                        	    statement;
                        	    iterator;
                            }
                        </pre>

                        This is also written as:
                        <pre>
                            variable = beginValue;
                            while (condition)
                            {
                        	    statement;
                        	    variable compound_assignment someValue;
                            }
                        </pre>
                        
                        where the: <br>
                        <code>while</code> <i>keyword</i> begins the While loop. It is <b>required.</b> <br>
                        <code>initializer</code> is the beginning value of the variable. It can be <b>optional.</b> <br>
                        <code>condition</code> is the condition required for the variable to reach the final value. It can be <b>optional.</b> <br>
                        <code>iterator</code> is the incremental or decremental value for the beginning value of the variable to reach the final value. 
                        It can be <b>optional.</b> <br><br>
                        Declare a variable and <i>initialize</i> it. <br>
                        <i>While</i> the <i>condition</i> is being met, execute the statement. <br><br>

                        (3.) <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/language-reference/keywords/do" target="_blank"><b>Do-While Loop</b></a> statement <br>
                        (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/language-reference/keywords/do) <br>
                        The <b>do-while</b> iteration statement executes a statement or a block of statements while a specified Boolean expression 
                        evaluates to true. <br>
                        It executes one or more times (<i>unlike the while loop that executes zero or more times</i>) because that expression is 
                        evaluated after each execution of the loop. <br>
                        The general syntax of a <b>do-while loop</b> is:
                        <pre>
                            initializer;
                            do 
                            {
                        	    statement;
                        	    iterator;
                            } 
                            while (condition);
                        </pre>

                        This is also written as:
                        <pre>
                            variable = beginValue;
                            do 
                            {
                        	    statement;
                        	    variable compound_assignment someValue;
                            } while (condition);
                        </pre>

                        where the: <br>
                        <code>do</code> <i>keyword</i> begins the Do-While loop. It is <b>required.</b> <br>
                        <code>initializer</code> is the beginning value of the variable. It can be <b>optional.</b> <br>
                        <code>condition</code> is the condition required for the variable to reach the final value. It can be <b>optional.</b> <br>
                        <code>iterator</code> is the incremental or decremental value for the beginning value of the variable to reach the final value. 
                        It can be <b>optional.</b> <br>
                        <code>while</code> <i>keyword</i> ensures that the condition is being met. It is <b>required.</b> <br><br>
                        Declare a variable and <i>initialize</i> it. <br>
                        <i>Do</i> the statement and iterate it <i>while</i> the <i>condition</i> is being met. <br><br>

                        Jump statements in action <br>
                        At any point within the <b>for loop</b>, the <b>while loop</b>, and the <b>do-while loop</b>: <br>
                        We can break out of the loop by using the <b>break</b> statement <br>
                        We can step to the next iteration in the loop using the <b>continue</b> statement <br>
                        We can exit the loop using the <b>goto</b> statement, <b>return</b> statement, 
                        or the exception handling statement: <b>throw</b> statement. <br><br>

                        (4.) <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/language-reference/keywords/foreach-in" target="_blank"><b>Foreach Loop</b></a> statement <br>
                        (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/language-reference/keywords/foreach-in) <br>
                        The <b>foreach</b> iteration statement executes a statement or a block of statements for each element in a collection (array) <br>
                        The general syntax of a <b>foreach loop</b> is:
                        <pre>
                            foreach (var item in collection)
                            {
                                statement;
                            }
                        </pre>

                        We shall implement the foreach statement in Module 7 (Arrays). <br><br>

                        We also have infinite while loop: while(true) <br>
                        It is the while statement used with the Boolean value of true <br>
                        We use the break statement to break the loop <br>
                        We shall discuss these kinds of loops later. <br><br>

                        Depending on the program, we can <b>nest <i>any statement</i> within <i>any statement</i></b> as needed. <br>
                        
                        Let us write some code examples <br>
                        Please review the screenshots and the comments. <br><br>
                        
                        Example 1: Print the even numbers less than $25$ <br>
                        This approach uses the Definition of an even number. It has a nested selection statement (if-statement). <br>
                        Use three different iteration statements. <br>

                        Example 3: Print the perfect squares between 1 and 100 (1 and 100 are inclusive) <br>
                        <img src="images/Module5CSharp/iteration-statements-3-1.png" alt="Iteration Statements 3-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/Module5CSharp/iteration-statements-3-2.png" alt="Iteration Statements 3-2"> <br>
                        <img src="images/Module5CSharp/iteration-statements-3-3.png" alt="Iteration Statements 3-3"> <br><br>
                        <i>
                            <b>Student:</b> It seems I do not understand this <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> What do you not understand? <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Initializing the variables <br>
                            Line #15 and Line #16: you initialized num1Squared and num2Squared <br>
                            Why did you not initilize num3Squared? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> I can...if I wanted to <br>
                            However, it is not necessary to do so. <br>
                            Line #20: I was going to use num1Squared (numSquared &lt; 100) <br>
                            If I did not initialize it before using it, it will be an error. <br>
                            The same applies to Line #31 (num2Squared &lt; 100) <br>
                            But, it does not apply to the do-while loop because in Line #45, it was defined with the formula <br>
                            We are supposed to define a variable after declaring that variable <br>
                            After defining it, then we can use it. <br>
                            But if we decide to use it after declaring it without defining it, there will be an error. <br>
                            So, it was not necessary for me to initialize num3Squared to 1 because I defined it in Line #45 before using it 
                            in Line #49 <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> So, initializing it is seen as defining it? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Yes...in that case/sense <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Another question <br>
                            The program prints the perfect squares between $1$ and $100$ (both ends inclusive) <br>
                            However, the condition in the program for those three different loops used less than 100 <br>
                            Should it not be less than or equal to 100? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Very good question <br>
                            Here's the thing: <br>
                            If we use less than or equal to 100, it will print $121$ and stop at $121$ rather than 100 <br>
                            The program is executed in a top-down approach <br>
                            So, the number will increment to 11 and print the square of 11 before the program ends. <br>
                            This is because 100 is less than or equal to 100 <br>
                            Based on the way we wrote the program, the number increments, then is squared, then the square is printed, and then
                            the condition (whether the square is less than 100) <br>
                            So, in order to print only that range (1 - 100), we want the program to stop at the condition when the square is 
                            less than 100. <br>
                            If the square is less than or equal to 100, it will continue, and then allow the number to increment to 11, square it to 
                            give 121, and the print it before that condition that stops it. We do not want it that way. <br>
                            Does it make sense? <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> I don't think so. <br>
                            Is there another way we can solve this question? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Yes of course. <br>
                            We can nest an if-statement inside the first two iteration statements: (for-loop and while-loop). <br>
                            In that case, we shall not initialize those variables. <br>
                            Also, we shall use the less than or equal to 100 in that approach. <br>
                            Let us write the program using that approach. 
                        </i> <br><br>

                <div id="Learn6" class="w3-container menu6 w3-padding white">
                    <h3>Vocabulary Words</h3>
                    <p>
                        method, function, main method, system pre-defined method, user-defined method, common language runtime (CLR), 
                        method signature, method name, method parameter, parameter list, method access, access level, public, private, 
                        abstract, sealed, return value, return type, void, method invocation, instance method, static method, 
                        argument, argument list, pass parameters by value, pass parameters by reference (<b>ref</b> keyword), 
                        parameter arrays, extension methods, async methods, expression-bodied members, expression body definitions, 
                        iterators, modifier, local variables, return keyword, value parameters, reference parameters, 
                        pass parameters by output (<b>out</b> keyword), pass parameters by input (<b>in</b> keyword),  
                        
                        
                    </p>
        
                    <h3>Objectives</h3>
                    <p>
                        Students will: <br><br>
                        (1.) Discuss methods. <br>
                        (2.) Pass parameters to methods (Pass by Value, Pass by Reference). <br>
                        (3.) Pass arguments to methods (Pass by Position, Pass by Name). <br>
                        (4.) Overload methods. <br>
                        (5.) Write programs that solves real-world problems using methods. 
                    </p>

                    <h3>Required Readings</h3>
        
                    <h3>Methods</h3>
                    <p>
                        <i>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Guess what? <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> We have been using a method since we wrote our first C# program. <br>
                            What is that method called? <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> You mean the <b>Main</b> method? <br>
                            The <b>static void Main(string[] args)</b> <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> That is correct. <br>
                            As we mentioned in the first module, every C# program is written in the <b>Main</b>
                            method. <br>
                            The <b>static void Main(string[] args)</b> method is the <b>system pre-defined main method</b> <br>
                            The methods we shall discuss in this module are <b>user-defined methods</b> <br>
                            The <b>Main</b> method is the entry point for every C# application, and it is called by the 
                            common language runtime (CLR) when the program is started. <br>                            
                            It is the only method that is called automatically regardless of the order in which it is
                            written. <br>
                            In other words, it does not matter if we write the <b>Main method</b> before or after the
                            <b>user-defined method</b> <br>
                            The <b>Main method</b> is called first by the C# program. <br>
                            This implies that any other <b>user-defined method</b> should be called in the <b>Main method</b>
                            directly or indirectly.
                            Note that we cannot use the reserved word, <b>Main</b> as the name of any method we define
                            (user-defined method). <br>                            
                        </i> <br><br>

                        A <b>method</b> is a block of code that contains a series of statements. <br>
                        It is also known as a <b>function.</b> <br>
                        It allows for reusability of those statements/codes, and perform some action when called. <br>
                        Reusability of codes: define one time, use many times. <br>
                        Anytime the method is called, the statements in that method are executed. <br><br>
                        
                        <b>Method Signature</b> is the <b>set of attibutes</b> used to <b>declare a method.</b> <br>
                        They consist of the: <br>
                        (1.)  <b>Access level:</b> such as public or private. <br>
                        By default, the access level is private. <br>
                        The access level is optional. <br><br>
                        (2.) <b>Modifier:</b> such as abstract or sealed. <br>
                        The modifier is optional. <br><br>
                        (3.) <b>Return value</b> or <b>void</b> <br>
                        Every method must have either a return value or a void. <br>
                        If the method has void, then it has no return value. <br><br>
                        (4.) <b>Method name:</b> the name of the method. <br>
                        It is important to use an appropriate identifier. <br>
                        Method names (identifiers) in C# begin with an uppercase letter (preferably Pascal casing) <br>
                        This is required. <br><br>
                        (5.) <b>Method parameters:</b> Method parameters are enclosed in parentheses. <br>
                        If the method has no parameter, then the parenthesis is empty. <br>
                        This implies that a parenthesis is required. <br>
                        If the method has parameters, the parameters are separated by commas. <br><br>

                        We shall discuss some of the method signatures in this module. <br>
                        We shall discuss the rest in the Advanced C# course. <br><br>

                        Usually, a method takes in <b>parameters.</b> <br> 
                        Parameters are normally variables, and we declare them as we declare variables. <br>
                        They are local variables to the method that takes them. <br>
                        
                        <i>
                            <b>Student:</b> To be honest, I have no idea what you just said. <br>
                            What are parameters? arguments? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Let us review this example.
                        </i> <br><br>

                        Assume we have this program with a user-defined method: <br>
                        <pre>
                        // Method
                        using System;
                    
                        namespace Methods
                        {
                            class Program
                            {
                                // Declare the method: SumNumbers 
                                // Parameters are: num1 and num2
                                public static int SumNumbers(int num1, int num2)
                                {
                                    return num1 + num2;
                                }
                        
                                static void Main(string[] args)
                                {
                                    // Call the method
                                    // Arguments are 5 and 7
                                    SumNumbers(5, 7);
                        
                                    // Print what you called
                                    Console.WriteLine(SumNumbers(5, 7));
                                }
                            }
                        }
                        </pre>

                        In user-defined method: <br>
                        In the <b>public static int SumNumbers(int num1, int num2)</b> <br>
                        <b>SumNumbers</b> is the name of the user-defined method <br>
                        <b>int</b> SumNumbers means that we want the method to return an integer <br>
                        When we declared the method: SumNumbers; we passed in two parameters to it. <br>
                        The <b>parameters</b> are: <b>num1</b> and <b>num2</b> <br>
                        Then we wrote a <b>return</b> statement that specifies what the method should return 
                        (the sum of the two parameter integers which also, is an integer) <br><br>
                        <i>
                            <b>Student:</b> Question...Mr. C <br>
                            Does it mean that we should know what the method should return before we declare it? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> That is right <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Can a method return more than one result? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> You are asking very good questions. <br>
                            Yes, it can. <br> 
                            We shall discuss Output parameters (passing paramters by output) later in this module. <br>
                            We use it if we want the user-defined method to return more than one value. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> What if we do not want it to return anything? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Then we shall declare it as void type rather than integer type <br>
                            We shall also review those cases in this module.                         
                        </i> <br><br>
                        <b>public</b> is the access level of the method. By default, it is private. This is optional. <br>
                        We shall discuss access levels later. <br>
                        For now, let us just use the <b>public</b> access level <br>
                        For this module, we can omit it if we want. But I prefer to use it. <br>
                        <b>static</b> means that it is a static method. <br>
                        A <b>static method</b> is a <b>method</b> that <b>belongs to a class</b> <u>but not</u> <b>an instance of the class.</b> <br>
                        A static method can only use and call other static methods. <br>
                        The <b>main method</b> is a <b>static method.</b> <br>
                        This implies that any user-defined method that we intend to call in the main method should also be a static method. <br>
                        There are still more details regarding static methods. However, we shall discuss those details in Object-oriented programming 
                        in the Advanced C# course. <br><br>
                        <i>
                            <b>Student:</b> What is an instance of the class? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> An instance of a class is called an object. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> What is an object? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> An object is an instance of a class. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> What is a class? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Take my Advanced C# course. We shall discuss Object-oriented programming in details. <br>
                            For now, let us just focus on Functional programming...programming based on functions (methods) <br>
                            In the Advanced C# course, we shall discuss Object-oriented programming...programming based on classes and objects.
                        </i> <br><br>
                        In the main method: <br>
                        <b>static void Main(string[] args)</b> <br>
                        We know that it is the main method because of the keyword, <b>Main</b> <br>
                        It is a static method because of the keyword, <b>static</b> <br>
                        It returns the void type because of the keyword <b>void</b> <br>
                        This means that it does not have any single return data type: Integer, Double, String, etc. <br>
                        However, it can return a statement that contains any or all of those return types in a String interpolation. <br>
                        The <b>arguments</b> are: <b>5</b> and <b>7</b> <br>
                        Then, we can print the value of the method we called on those arguments using the Console.WriteLine(); statement. <br>
                        We can also assign an Integer data type variable to that method and just print the variable. <br>
                        This is what I mean:
                        <code>
                            int result = SumNumbers(5, 7); <br>
                            Console.WriteLine(result);
                        </code> <br>
                        The code will give the same output. <br>
                        So: <br>
                        when we declare a method, we use parameters. <br>
                        when we call that method, we use arguments. <br>
                        A <b>Parameter</b> represents the value that the method expects you to provide when you call it. <br>
                        The declaration of the method defines its parameters. <br>
                        A method can be defined with no parameter, one parameter, or several parameters. <br>
                        A <b>Parameter List</b> is the part of the method definition that specifies the parameters. <br><br>
                        An <b>Argument</b> represents the value you provide to a method parameter when you call that method. <br>
                        The calling code provides the arguments when it calls the method. <br>
                        The arguments must be compatible with their respective parameter types. <br>
                        An <b>Argument List</b> is the part of the method call that specifies the arguments. <br><br>
                        To create a method that does not return a value (though can return multiple values in String Interpolation), we use the 
                        void keyword <br>
                        To create a method that returns a value of a specific data type, we declare that method with that data type. <br><br>
                        <i>
                            <b>Student:</b> Which one should we use? void or the specific data type? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> It depends. <br>
                            To return multiple values in a String Interpolation, the void keyword is needed. <br>
                            An alternative is to pass the parameters by output using the <b>out</b> keyword (part of passing by reference). <br>
                            Another alternative is to use Tuples (which we shall discuss in the next module: Module 7) <br>
                            To return a single value, you can use any of them: void or the data type. <br>
                            Let us review some code examples. <br>
                            Then, you can decide which one you would like to use for any program. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Guess we have several ways of passing parameters? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Yes, we do. <br>
                            We shall discuss those ways later in this module. 
                        </i> <br><br>

                        <b>Declaration syntax:</b> The syntax for declaring a Void type method is: <br>
                        <code>
                            [accessLevel] static void MethodName(parameterList) <br>
                            { <br>
                                &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; statements; <br>
                                &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Console.WriteLine($"something"); <br>
                            }
                        </code> <br><br>

                        <b>Declaration syntax:</b> The syntax for declaring a Data type method is: <br>
                        <code>
                            [accessLevel] static dataType MethodName(parameterList) <br>
                            { <br>
                                &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; statements; <br>
                                &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; return something; <br>
                            }                            
                        </code>
                    </p>

                        $
                        fahrenheitTemp = \dfrac{9 * celsiusTemp}{5} + 32 \\[5ex]

                            I would prefer to use the variable: fahrenheitTemp rather than 98.6 <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> I prefer the void method too <br>
                            It helps if we want to return multiple values along with a message in a String Interpolation <br>

                        </i>
