                    <h4>Why Are We Learning Methods?</h4>
                    <p>
                        (1.) <b>Program Requirement</b> <br>
                        Every line of executable code in any C# application must be inside some method. <br><br>

                        (2.) <b>Reusability: write one time, use several times</b> <br>
                        Methods are useful for performing repeated or shared tasks such as frequently used calculations, text and control 
                        manipulations, and database operations. <br>
                        We can just define a method one time, and then reuse the statements inside the method as many times as we want 
                        whenever we call that method. <br><br>

                        (3.) <b>Easier Code Readability</b> and <b>Faster Debugging</b> <br>
                        Imagine a "big" program (program with long lines of codes). <br>
                        Methods allow such programs to be broken into discrete logical units. <br>
                        This helps for easier code readability and faster debugging. <br><br>

                        (4.) <b>Avoiding Code Duplication</b> <br>
                        After developing a method in one program, it can be reused in another program with little or no modifcation. 
                    </p>
                    
                    <h4>Passing Parameters: By Value or By Reference</h4>
                    <p>
                        There are <b>two ways</b> to <b>pass parameters</b> to a <b>method in C#</b> <br>
                        They are: <br>
                        (1.) Pass by Value (Value Parameters) <br>
                        In the previous examples we did for the Void type and Data type methods; we passed parameters by value because the 
                        default passing mechanism is to pass parameters by value. <br><br>
                        (2.) Pass by Reference (Reference Parameters) <br>
                        When we pass parameters by reference, we use the <b>ref</b> keyword before the data type in the parameter variable as well 
                        as before its corresponding argument. <br><br>

                        As we learned earlier: <br>
                        When we declare a method, we use parameters. <br>
                        When we call the method, we use arguments. <br><br>
                        
                        In C#, a paramter can be passed to a method <b>by value</b> or <b>by reference</b> <br>
                        <b>Passing Mechanism</b> is the passing of a parameter to a method by value or by reference. <br>
                        It determines whether the method can modify the programming element underlying the argument in the calling code. <br>
                        Please note that passing mechanism is optional in method declarations. <br>
                        <b>The default passing mechanism in C# (when the passing mechanism is not specified) is to <u>pass parameters by value.</u></b>
                    </p>

                    <h5>Choice of Passing Mechanism</h5>
                    <table>
                        <caption><b>By Value</b> or <b>By Reference</b>?</caption>
                        <tr>
                            <th class="w3-center green" style="width: 50%">Passing Parameter By Value</th>
                            <th class="w3-center green" style="width: 50%">Passing Parameter By Reference (<b>ref</b> keyword)</th>
                        </tr>
                        <tr>
                            <td>
                                (1.) <b>Protection:</b> Exposure of calling variables to change <br>
                                The advantage of passing a parameter by value is that it protects a variable from being changed by the method. 
                            </td>
                            <td>
                                (1.) <b>Protection:</b> Exposure of calling variables to change <br>
                                The advantage of passing a parameter by reference is that the method can return a value to the calling code 
                                through that argument. 
                            </td>
                        </tr>
                        <tr>
                            <td>
                                (2.) <b>Performance:</b> For a value data type, C# copies the entire data contents of the 
                                argument. <br>
                                This implies that for a large value type such as a structure, it can be less efficient to pass it by value. <br><br>
                                For a reference data type, C# copies only the pointer to the data 
                                (which is four bytes on 32-bit platforms and eight bytes on 64-bit platforms). <br>
                                So, performance is not an issue when an argument is passed by value for a reference data type. 
                            </td>
                            <td>
                                (2.) <b>Performance:</b> For a value data type, C# copies the entire data contents of the 
                                argument. <br>
                                This implies that for a large value type such as a structure, it can be more efficient to pass it by value. <br><br>
                                For a reference data type, C# copies only the pointer to the data 
                                (which is four bytes on 32-bit platforms and eight bytes on 64-bit platforms). <br>
                                So, performance is not an issue when an argument is passed by reference for a reference data type. 
                            </td>
                        </tr>
                        <tr>
                            <td>
                                (3.) If the calling code element underlying the argument is a nonmodifiable element, declare the corresponding parameter 
                                by value. <br>
                                No code can change the value of a nonmodifiable element.
                            </td>
                            <td>
                                (3.) If the procedure needs to change the underlying element in the calling code, declare the corresponding 
                                parameter by reference.
                            </td>
                        </tr>
                        <tr>
                            <td>
                                (4.) If the underlying element is modifiable, but you do not want the procedure to be able to change its value; 
                                declare the parameter by value. <br>
                                Only the calling code can change the value of a modifiable element passed by value. 
                            </td>
                            <td>
                                (4.) If the correct execution of the code depends on the procedure changing the underlying element in the calling 
                                code, declare the parameter by reference. 
                            </td>
                        </tr>
                    </table>

                    <p>
                        Definitions: <br>
                        A <b>local variable</b> is a <b>variable declared within a method.</b> <br>
                        A <b>member variable</b> is a <b>variable declared inside a class or structure</b>, 
                        but not within any method internal to that class or structure. <br>
                        We shall focus on only local variables in this module. <br><br>

                        <i>
                            <b>Student:</b> Mr. C, if I say I did not understand the passing mechanism you just explained, I mean it. <br>
                            I want to learn and understand it, rather than just memorize. <br>
                            Is there any way you can break it down? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Yes, let's do some examples. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Alright, but please use a simple example. <br>
                            You have been using Mathematics examples. <br>
                            Can you explain this time with English...say using Strings? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Okay. Not a problem. Let's do the examples.
                        </i> <br><br>

                        Pass Parameters by Value: Example $1$ <br>
                        <img src="images/Module6CSharp/pass-parameters-by-value-1.png" alt="Pass Parameters by Value 1"> <br><br>

                        Pass Parameters by Reference: Example $1$ <br>
                        <img src="images/Module6CSharp/pass-parameters-by-reference-1.png" alt="Pass Parameters by Reference 1"> <br><br>

                        <i>
                            <b>Student:</b> So, when we pass parameters by value, the arguments are printed; and when we pass paramaters by 
                            reference, the parameters (rather than the arguments) are printed. <br>
                            Is that correct? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> That is correct. <br>
                            When we pass parameters by value (which is the default passing mechanism when the passing mechanism is not specified),
                            the copies of the parameters (which is the arguments) are printed. <br>
                            The actual parameters are not affected. <br>
                            Only the arguments (which are the copies of the parameters) are printed. <br>
                            When we pass parameters by reference, the actual parameters are affected because the actual parameters become associated 
                            with the arguments passed to the function. <br>
                            Would you like for us to do another example? <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Yes please. <br>
                            But keep it simple. <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Okay. Let's do an example with numbers. 
                        </i> <br><br>

                        Pass Parameters by Value: Example $2$ <br>
                        <img src="images/Module6CSharp/pass-parameters-by-value-2.png" alt="Pass Parameters by Value 2"> <br><br>

                        Pass Parameters by Reference: Example $2$ <br>
                        <img src="images/Module6CSharp/pass-parameters-by-reference-2.png" alt="Pass Parameters by Reference 2"> <br><br>

                        <i>
                            <b>Student:</b> I get it now. <br>
                            But, what if we decided to print the values in the user-defined method rather than the main method? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Then the program will print the parameters in that user-defined method <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Regardless of the passing mechanism? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Yes... <br>
                            Provided you did not print anything in the Main Method <br>
                            So, this is what happens <br>
                            When the user-defined method is called in the main method, the program will head to the user-defined method and execute every 
                            statement there. <br>
                            So it will print whatever it sees there <br>
                            And head back to the main method <br>
                            And if it sees there is no print command in the main method, it will not print anything on the console. 
                        </i> <br><br>

                        <b>Parameters passed by Reference</b> can further be <b>passed by output</b> or <b>passed by input</b> <br>
                        Let us discuss them.  
                    </p>

                    <br>

                    <table>
                        <caption>Compare and Contrast Passing by: Reference (ref), Output (out), and Input (in)</caption>
                        <tr>
                            <th class="green w3-center" style="width:34%">Passing by Reference (ref)</th>
                            <th class="green w3-center" style="width: 33%">Passing by Output (out)</th>
                            <th class="green w3-center" style="width: 33%">Passing by Input (in)</th>
                        </tr>
                        <tr>
                            <td>
                                (1.)  It makes the formal parameter an alias for the argument, which must be a variable. <br>
                                In other words, any operation on the parameter is made on the argument.
                            </td>
                            <td>
                                (1.) It is a passing by reference. <br>
                                It makes the formal parameter an alias for the argument, which must be a variable. <br>
                                In other words, any operation on the parameter is made on the argument.
                            </td>
                            <td>
                                (1.) It is a passing by reference. <br>
                                It makes the formal parameter an alias for the argument, which must be a variable. <br>
                                In other words, any operation on the parameter is made on the argument.
                            </td>
                        </tr>
                        <tr>
                            <td>
                                (2.) Requires that the variables passed in as arguments be initialized before being passed in a method call. <br>
                                However, the called method may assign a value or modify the argument. (See Number (3.))
                            </td>
                            <td>
                                (2.) Does not require the variables passed in as arguments to be initialized before being passed in a method call.
                            </td>
                            <td>
                                (2.) Requires that the variables passed in as arguments be initialized before being passed in a method call. <br>
                                However, the called method may not assign a value or modify the argument. (See Number (3.))
                            </td>
                        </tr>
                        <tr>
                            <td>
                                (3.) Allows the called method to modify the argument value. <br>
                                <b>ref</b> arguments <u>may</u> be modified. 
                            </td>
                            <td>
                                (3.) The called method must modify the argument value. <br>
                                <b>out</b> arguments <u>must</u> be modified by the called method. 
                            </td>
                            <td>
                                (3.) Does not allow the called method to modify the argument value. <br>
                                <b>in</b> arguments <u>cannot</u> be modified by the called method. 
                            </td>
                        </tr>
                        <tr>
                            <td>
                                (4.) Both the method definition (declaration) and the calling method must explicitly use the <b>ref</b> keyword. 
                            </td>
                            <td>
                                (4.) Both the method definition (declaration) and the calling method must explicitly use the <b>out</b> keyword. 
                            </td>
                            <td>
                                (4.) The method definition (declaration) must use the <b>in</b> keyword. However, the calling method must not 
                                use it.  
                            </td>
                        </tr>
                        <tr>
                            <td colspan="3">
                                The <b>ref</b>, <b>out</b>, and <b>in</b> keywords are not part of the method signature for the purpose of 
                                overload resolution. <br>
                                This implies that methods cannot be overloaded if the only difference is that one method takes a <b>ref</b> or 
                                <b>in</b> argument and the othee takes an <b>out</b> argument. 
                            </td>
                        </tr>
                    </table>


                    
                    <h4>Passing Arguments: By Position and By Name</h4>
                    <p>
                        We have seen the two ways we can pass parameters. <br>
                        Let us discuss the two ways we can pass arguments. <br>
                        When we call a Method, we can pass the arguments <b>by position</b> or <b>by name</b> <br>
                        In the examples we did so far, we passed the arguments by position. <br>
                        <b>Passing Arguments by Position</b> is passing the arguments in the order in which they appear in the method's 
                        declaration when we defined the parameters. <br>
                        It is delimited (separated) by commas <br>
                        You can omit an argument when you call the method if you made it optional with the corresponding parameter. <br>
                        However, if you do; it's place must be held with a comma. <br><br>
                        <b>Passing Arguments by Name</b> is passing the arguments without regard to position. <br>
                        They can be passed in any order. However, we must specify the 
                        <b>name of the parameter</b> followed by <b>a colon</b> followed by the <b>value of the argument</b> 
                        (parameterName: argumentValue) <br>
                        You can omit an argument when you call the procedure if you made it optional with the corresponding parameter. <br>
                        But the nice thing about this approach is that you do not have to use commas to denote missing positional arguments. <br>
                        Another nice feature about this approach is that you do not have to follow the order in which the parameters were defined 
                        in the method. <br>
                        Further, this approach makes it easier to note which arguments you are passing and which ones you are omitting. <br>
                        Remember when we used this approach... in Module 4 when we covered Date and Time methods <br>
                        Let us write some code examples. <br><br>

                        Pass Arguments by Position Example <br>
                        <img src="images/Module6CSharp/pass-arguments-by-position.png" alt="Pass Arguments by Position"> <br><br>

                        Pass Arguments by Name Example <br>
                        <img src="images/Module6CSharp/pass-arguments-by-name.png" alt="Pass Arguments by Name">
                    </p>

                    <h3>Required Readings</h3>
                    <h3>Array</h3>
                    <p>
                        An <b>Array</b> is a <b>list</b> or <b>data container</b> that contains: <br>
                        (1.) single or multiple <b>values</b> of the <b>same data type</b> or <br>
                        (2.) multiple <b>values</b> of <b>different data types</b> <br><br>

                        The <b>dimension</b> of an array is the <b>direction in which one can specify the elements of the array.</b> <br>
                        Example: <br>
                        Record the ages of the students in Mr C's class. <br>
                        We have only one variable: age. <br>
                        This is a one-dimensional array...array of integers <br>
                        A one-dimensional array is also known as a single-dimensional array. <br><br>

                        Each student in Mr. C's class takes at least one course this semester. <br>
                        Record the number of courses taken by each student in Mr. C's class. <br>
                        Some of the courses are one-credit, two-credit, three-credit, four-credit courses. <br>
                        Assume the tuition for each course depends on the number of credit units. <br>
                        Calculate the total cost of all the courses taken by all the students in Mr. C's class. <br>
                        For us to do this, we need to list the number of courses taken by each student in Mr. C's class. We can represent this in 
                        the column. <br>
                        We also need to calculate the cost of the courses taken by each student. We can represent this in the row. <br>
                        So, we have two variables: courses and cost. <br>
                        This is a two-dimensional array. <br>
                        The column is the array of integers ... the number of courses. <br>
                        The row is the array of doubles ... the cost of the number of courses taken by each student. <br>
                        We can then find the total cost...<i>connect this explanation with <b>Matrices</b> in Algebra</i> <br>
                        In this module, we are only concerned with one-dimensional array. <br><br>

                        The <b>rank</b> of an array is the <b>number of dimensions</b> in the array. <br>
                        This implies that the rank of a one-dimensional array is 1 <br>
                        The rank of a two-dimensional array is 2 <br>
                        The rank of a three-dimensioanl array is 3 and so on and so forth. <br><br>

                        <b>Why Are We Learning Arrays?</b> <br>
                        Assume you want to work with five different integers; rather than declare five different
                        variables for the five integers, it is much better to declare a single integer array that will contain all five
                        integers. <br><br>
                        
                        When we declare an array in C#: <br>
                        (1.) We must declare the data type of the array. <br>
                        (2.) We may or may not not indicate the size of the array. <br><br>
                    
                        <b>Notable Notes About Arrays</b> <br>
                        (1.) An array is declared by specifying the data type of its elements. <br>
                        You may or may not write the elements when you declare the array. <br>
                        If the <b>elements are of the same data type</b>, then the <b>array is declared by specifying that data type</b>. <br>
                        If the <b>elements are of different data types</b>, the the <b>array is declared by specifying <i>object</i> as the data type.</b> <br>
                        When the elements are specified, they are enclosed in curly brackets or braces. <br><br>

                        (2.) If the elements are of the same data type or of different data types, the <b>var</b> keyword can also be used. <br>
                        The <b>new</b> keyword is used to specify the elements of an array if the array was declared without the elements. <br>
                        Let us review several ways of declaring arrays. 
                        <pre>
                            /*
                                elements are not specified
                                elements are the same data type
                            */
                            dataType[] arrayName; 

                            /*
                                elements are not specified
                                elements are the same data type
                                array size is specified
                            */
                            dataType[] arrayName = new dataType[arraySize]; 

                            /*
                                elements are specified, separated by comma   
                                elements are the same data type
                            */
                            dataType[] arrayName = {array elements};

                            /*
                                elements are specified, separated by comma   
                                elements are the same data type
                                array size is specified
                            */
                            dataType[] arrayName = new dataType[arraySize] {array elements};

                            /*
                                elements are not specified
                                elements are of different data types
                            */
                            object[] arrayName;

                            /*
                                elements are not specified
                                elements are of different data types
                                array size is specified
                            */
                            object[] arrayName = new object[arraySize]; 

                            /*
                                elements are specified, separated by comma
                                elements are of different data types
                            */
                            object[] arrayName = {array elements};

                            /*
                                elements are specified, separated by comma   
                                elements are of different data types
                                array size is specified
                            */
                            object[] arrayName = new object[arraySize] {array elements};

                            /*
                                elements are specified
                                elements are of the same or different data types
                            */
                            var arrayName = new[] {array elements};

                            /*
                                elements are specified
                                elements are of the same or different data types
                                array size is specified
                            */
                            var arrayName = new[arraySize] {array elements};
                        </pre>
 
                        (3.) When an array is created, the number of dimensions and the length of each dimension are established. <br>
                        Once the array instance is created, the number of dimensions and the length of each dimension cannot be changed during the 
                        lieftime of the instance. <br><br>
                        (4.) Arrays are zero-indexed. <br>
                        This implies that the position of array elements begin with zero. <br>
                        The first element in the array is at position: zero or index: zero <br>
                        The second array element is at index: one <br>
                        The index of the third element in the array is two. <br>
                        The index of the fourth array element is three. <br>
                        <b>Given an array of size: $n$; the index of the last element in the array is $n - 1$</b> <br>
                        We output the index of the array element by: <br>
                        <code>array[index] = element;</code> <br><br>
                    
                        Example 1: Array of the perfect squares of the first positive ten integers <br> 
                        Declare an array of a specified size without the elements <br>
                        Include elements in the array by using array index <br>
                        Print the length/size of the array using the <b>arrayName.Length</b> method <br>
                        Print the array using the different types of iteration statements: for loop, while loop, do-while loop, and foreach loop <br>
                        <img src="images/Module7CSharp/arrays-example-1-1.png" alt="Example 1-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/Module7CSharp/arrays-example-1-2.png" alt="Example 1-2"> <br>
                        <img src="images/Module7CSharp/arrays-example-1-3.png" alt="Example 1-3"> <br>
                        <img src="images/Module7CSharp/arrays-example-1-4.png" alt="Example 1-4"> <br>
                        <img src="images/Module7CSharp/arrays-example-1-5.png" alt="Example 1-5"> <br><br>

                        <i>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Remember we mentioned the <b>foreach loop</b> in Module 6 <br>
                            And we said we will implement it in Module 7 <br>
                            Do you like it? <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Yes I prefer it. <br>
                            It seems easier than the other statements. <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Well, we shall be using it as we see fit. <br>
                            Let us briefly discuss it. 
                        </i>
                    </p>

                    <table>
                        <tr>
                            <th class="green w3-center" style="width: 50%">ForEach Loop</th>
                            <th class="green w3-center" style="width: 50%">For Loop, While Loop, Do-While Loop</th>
                        </tr>
                        <tr>
                            <td>(1.) Loops through each array element without using a counter.</td>
                            <td>(1.) Loops through each array element using a counter.</td>
                        </tr>
                        <tr>
                            <td>(2.) The length of the array is not needed.</td>
                            <td>(2.) The length of the array is needed.</td>
                        </tr>
                    </table>
        
                    <p>
                        Example 2: Create an array of strings of size 4 <br>

                    <h4>Data Analysis: Descriptive Statistics with C#</h4>
                    <p>         
                        Statistics comprises Data Analysis. <br> 
                        Data Analysis is part of Data Science. <br>
                        Data Science is part of Computer Science/Programming. <br>
                        As a programmer, it is very likely that you will deal with data. <br>
                        In this course, we shall focus only on some of the Descriptive Statistics of Data Analysis. <br><br>

                        The <a href="https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/descriptive-statistics/" target="_blank">Descriptive Statistics</a> that we shall discuss in this course are: <br><br>
                        
                        (1.) <a href="https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/descriptive-statistics/#introductionMeasuresCenter" target="_blank">Measures of Center</a>:
                        Also known as the Measures of Central Tendency <br>
                        They are the: Mean, Median, Mode, and Midrange. <br><br>

                        (2.) <a href="https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/descriptive-statistics/#introductionMeasuresSpread" target="_blank">Measures of Spread</a>:
                        Also known as the Measures of Dispersion or the Measures of Variability or the Measures of Variation <br>
                        For this course, we shall focus on the: Range, Variance, and Standard Deviation. <br><br>                  
                        
                        (3.) <a href="https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/descriptive-statistics/#introductionMeasuresPosition" target="_blank">Measures of Position</a>:
                        Also known as the Measures of Location <br>
                        For this course, we shall focus on the: Five Number Summary of Data <br>
                        They are the: Minimum, First Quartile, Second Quartile, Third Quartile, and Maximum. <br>
                        The First Quartile is also known as the Lower Quartile or the 25th Percentile <br>
                        The Second Quartile is also known as the Median or the Middle Quartile or 50th Percentile <br>
                        The Third Quartile is also known as the Upper Quartile or 75th Percentile <br><br>

                        C# has some predefined methods used for calculating some of these measures. <br>
                        We shall calculate the rest of the measures. <br>
                        Let us write program examples of descriptive statistics using C# <br>
                        The formulas used are the formulas on the Descriptive Statistics website <br>
                        (1.) <a href="https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/descriptive-statistics/#formulasMeasuresCenter" target="_blank">Formulas for the Measures of Center</a> <br>
                        (2.) <a href="https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/descriptive-statistics/#formulasMeasuresSpread" target="_blank">Formulas for the Measures of Variation</a> <br>
                        (3.) <a href="https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/descriptive-statistics/#formulasMeasuresPosition" target="_blank">Formulas for the Measures of Location</a> <br><br>

                        Example 4: Data Analysis: Descriptive Statistics of Raw Ungrouped Data with C# <br>
                        <img src="images/Module7CSharp/arrays-example-4-1.png" alt="Example 4-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/Module7CSharp/arrays-example-4-2.png" alt="Example 4-2"> <br>
                        <img src="images/Module7CSharp/arrays-example-4-3.png" alt="Example 4-3"> <br>
                        <img src="images/Module7CSharp/arrays-example-4-4.png" alt="Example 4-4"> <br>
                        <img src="images/Module7CSharp/arrays-example-4-5.png" alt="Example 4-5"> <br>
                        <img src="images/Module7CSharp/arrays-example-4-6.png" alt="Example 4-6"> <br>
                        <img src="images/Module7CSharp/arrays-example-4-7.png" alt="Example 4-7"> <br>
                        <img src="images/Module7CSharp/arrays-example-4-8.png" alt="Example 4-8"> <br>

                        This determines the length (size) of the array. <br><br>
                        Line #26: we used the system-defined Array class method: <code>Array.Sort</code> which takes in the user-defined array: 
                        <code>dataset</code> as the argument. <br>

                        For Line #182 <br>
                        This is another implementation of the methods of the Array class. <br>

                        <a href="https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/descriptive-statistics/#formulasMeasuresPosition" target="_blank">this formula</a>,
                        we had to sort the array in descending order.

                    <h4>GUI Programming: Part 1: Descriptive Analysis with C#</h4>
                    <p>
                        <b>ListBox, Buttons, RichTextBox, Another C# Form</b> <br>
                        In previous modules, we explored the use of Button and RichTextBox <br>
                        In this module, let us explore the use of the ListBox <br><br>

                        The <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.windows.controls.listbox?view=net-5.0" target="_blank">ListBox</a> is a toolbox control that <b>contains a list of selectable items.</b> <br>
                        The ListBox accepts only the string data type by default (in a String Collection Editor), just like the TextBox and 
                        the ComboBox. <br>
                        However, we will need to convert the ListBox into an Array. <br>
                        To do this, we need to cast the data in the ListBox to a string, then to an Array. <br>
                        The code used to do this is: <br>
                        <code>var data = DatasetListBox.Items.Cast&lt;string&gt;().ToArray();</code> <br>
                        Notice we used the <i>var</i> keyword to declare the variable: <i>data</i> <br> 
                        Say we want to work with integers, we will then need to convert the array of strings into an array of integers. <br>
                        The code used to convert an array of strings to an array of integers is: <br>
                        <code>int[] dataset = data.Select(int.Parse).ToArray();</code> <br><br>                        
                        Items can be entered into the ListBox directly in the Design Form <u>or</u> from the Code window <u>or</u> from the Button 
                        and another Toolbox Control. <br>
                        Let us enter the items directly. <br>
                        Let us do Question (13.) on the <a href="https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/descriptive-statistics/measuresCenter.html" target="_blank">Solved Examples on the Measures of Center</a> website <br>
                        <img src="images/Module7CSharp/listbox-1.png" alt="ListBox 1"> <br><br>
                        <img src="images/Module7CSharp/listbox-2.png" alt="ListBox 2"> <br><br>
                        We are not using Data Bound Items so we leave that box unchecked. <br><br>

                        In this example, we shall: <br>
                        (1.) Use a ListBox to include the elements of the array <br>
                        (2.) Use Buttons to respond to user-event to calculate the descriptive statistics (each of the measures) <br>
                        (3.) Write methods (user-defined methods only) to calculate the descriptive statistics. <br>
                        (4.) Call each method under each button event accordingly. <br>
                        (5.) Use a RichTextBox in another Form to display the results.
                    </p>

                        <i>
                            <b>Student:</b> I have several questions. <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Okay, go ahead. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> First: why did you declare doubles when the dataset contains only integers? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Doubles take care of integers... well if you do not declare Option Strict On <br>
                            Here is what I like about C# and VB (something Java does not do...unfortunately) <br>
                            If you declare the integers and the answer as doubles, and the answer ends up being an integer; C# and VB.NET will 
                            display them as integers. <br>
                            So, C# and VB will accept them as doubles (even though they are integers) and will also dsiplay the answer as an 
                            integer if the answer is an integer (even though you declared the answer as a double) <br>
                            On the other hand, Java will accept them as doubles and will also display the answer as a double. Java will also throw 
                            a fit if you declare them as doubles and declare the answer as an integer. In that case, it will require you to convert 
                            it (cast it) as an integer. <br>
                            So, when working with VB and C#; it is very okay if you declare the data and the answer as doubles even if they are 
                            integers. <br>
                            Besides, what if you have a mixture of integers and doubles in the dataset? <br>
                            It is much safer to declare them as doubles. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> It seems Java is unforgiving <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> It is not just unforgiving of mistakes. It is <u>very</u> unforgiving of mistakes. <br>
                            It is very particular about everything. <br> 
                            <b>Student:</b> VB seems to be the really nice one. <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> VB is very nice. <br>
                            However, it is lenient to a fault. <br>
                            For example; you can declare numbers as integers enclosed in quotations marks, and add those numbers in VB. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Really? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Yes <br>
                            VB will think you probably made a mistake by enclosing the numbers in quotation marks and will go ahead, treat them 
                            as numbers, and add them. <br>
                            That is definitely not good. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> I guess that makes C# the win-win <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Hmmmm... I might disagree <br>
                            All programming langauges have their pros and cons. <br>
                            For someone who wants to learn a friendly lanaguage, the person should begin with VB <br>
                            However, if the person intends to learn other languages such as C, C++, C#, Java; the person should probably not begin with
                            VB <br>
                            VB syntax is very different from C# and Java. <br>
                            The person should begin with C# <br>
                            This is because C# "falls in the middle" of VB and Java <br>
                            C# has some similar methods with VB because of the .NET configuration. It also has similar syntax with Java. <br>
                            Anyone who learns C# will easily learn VB and will not struggle with learning Java <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> What if the person begins with Java? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Then the person should be determined not to quit. <br>
                            What is your next question? <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> In this example, you used the in-built Average() method to get the mean. <br>
                            But you used a for-loop in the console application to get the mean. <br>
                            You decided to do things differently? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Very good observation. <br>
                            Notice Line #6 in the desktop application: <b><i>using System.Linq;</i></b> <br>
                            It has the pre-defined method to calculate the average <br>
                            It is automatically included in the desktop application but not in the console application <br>
                            To use it in the console application, we have to include it by writing the command: <b><i>using System.Linq;</i></b> <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> My next question... <br>                            
                            What if the user decides to enter the data? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> You mean: a user-entered data rather than an already-entered (pre-defined) data? <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Yes Sir <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Let us do the example. <br>
                            Would you like for us to begin with a console application or a desktop application for the example? <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Let us begin with a desktop application because we are already discussing it. <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Okay. <br>
                            In that case, we shall need a MultiLine TextBox <br>
                            It is actually the regular TextBox. <br>
                            Remember, the TextBox enables the user to enter text. <br>
                            Further, it provides multiline editing and password character masking. <br>
                            In this case, we shall check the box that says MultiLine. <br>
                            Let's do it.                          
                        </i>

                    <h4>GUI Programming: Part 2: Descriptive Analysis with C#</h4>
                    <p>
                        <b>MultiLine TextBox, Buttons, Another C# Form, RichTextBox</b> <br>
                        When the data needs to be entered by the user, rather than an-already entered data; then the MultiLine TextBox should be 
                        used. <br>
                        The MultiLine TextBox is a TextBox. We just have to check the MultiLine option. <br><br>
                        By default, it accepts only the string data type...it is a TextBox after all <br>
                        If you review the Properties window of the MultiLine TextBox, you will notice that the <b>Lines</b> property is a 
                        <b>String[] Array.</b> It is an array of strings. <br>
                        Say we want to work with an array of integers: <br>
                        We will need to convert that array of strings into an array of integers. <br>
                        The code used to convert an array of strings to the array of integers is: <br>
                        <code>int[] arrayIntegers = Array.ConvertAll(MultiLineTextBox.Lines, new Converter<string, int>(int.Parse));</code> <br><br>

                        In this example, we shall: <br>
                        (1.) Use a MultiLine TextBox to allow the user to enter values of any type in new lines <br>
                        (2.) Use Buttons to respond to user-event to calculate the descriptive statistics (each of the measures) <br>
                        (3.) Write methods (user-defined methods only) to calculate the descriptive statistics. <br>
                        (4.) Call each method under each button event accordingly. <br>
                        (5.) Use a RichTextBox in another Form to display the results.
                    </p>

                    <h5>Design User Interface and Set Properties</h5>
                    <p>
                        <b>Form1.cs:</b> <i>Adjust and Align</i> <br>
                        <b>(Name)</b> property: <i>Leave as is. Do not change</i> <br>
                        <b>Text</b> property: Descriptive Statistics (User will enter data) <br>
                        <b>BackColor</b> property: Web: Lavender <br>
                        <b>Font:</b> property: Font: Times New Roman; &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Font Style: Regular; &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Size: 18 <br>
                        <b>FormBorderStyle</b> property: Sizable <i>as is</i> <br>
                        <b>MinimizeBox</b> property: True <i>as is</i> <br>
                        <b>MaximizeBox</b> property: True <i>as is</i> <br><br>
                        <i>
                            We want the user to be able to adjust the application to accommodate the sample size of the data. 
                        </i> <br><br>

                        <b>TextBox:</b> <i>Adjust and Align</i> <br>

                    <h5>Code our Program (Write the Program Code)</h5>
                    <p>
                        (1.) Click the View menu and click the <em>Code tab View</em> &rightarrow; <em>Code</em>, or press the F7 function key on your Windows keyboard 
                        or double-click any empty space on the form in the <b>Design window.</b> <br>
                        This leads to the <b>Code window</b> <br>
                        Scroll a little bit to the top, write the comments, and begin to... <br><br>
                    
                        (2.) Write the code. <br>
                        We shall write methods for calculating the Measures of Center, Measures of Spread, and the Measures of Position. <br>
                        Then, we call the method respectively when the user clicks the button for each of them. <br>
                        For example: <br>
                        When the user clicks the Measures of Center button, the method for calculating the measures of center is called, the measures 
                        of center is calculated, and displayed in the RichTextBox on the second form. <br>
                        When the user clicks the Measures of Spread button, the method for calculating the measures of spread is called, the measures 
                        of spread is calculated, and displayed in the RichTextBox on the second form. <br>
                        When the user clicks the Measures of Position button, the method for calculating the measures of position is called, the measures 
                        of position is calculated, and displayed in the RichTextBox on the second form. <br>
                        When the user clicks the Descriptive Statistics button, the methods for calculating all the measures are called, all the measures 
                        are calculated, and displayed in the RichTextBox on the second form. <br><br>

                        Let us do Question (45.) on the <a href="https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/descriptive-statistics/measuresCenter.html" target="_blank">Solved Examples on the Measures of Center</a> website <br>
                        Remember that this application with the MultiLine TextBox will allow the user to enter the data values directly. <br>
                        Please review the screenshots. <br>
                        Review the comments. <br><br>

                            <b>Student:</b> Excuse me, Mr. C <br>
                            This course is only for C# <br>
                            What do you mean? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> You are correct. <br>
                            But, these subsequent questions are not for everyone. <br>
                            That is the reason I am putting them at the end. <br>
                            I have a question for you. <br>
                            Have you ever seen any IT/Computer Science position that requires the knowledge of only one programming language? <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> I have not checked, Sir. <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> If you intend to be in the IT/Programming field for the long run, at least four (four or more) 
                            languages are required. <br>
                            If you look for IT positions at Indeed.com or other career/job websites, you will realize what I mean. <br>
                            Search for <b>c# programmer</b>, you might be surprised that even for entry level positions 
                            for a C# Programmer; they require you to have a working knowledge of several other programming languages. <br>
                            Mr. C is preparing you for that...if you intend to work in the programming field for a long time. <br>
                            Besides, this is a critical thinking approach. <br>
                            I am required to develop and promote critical thinking questions for my students. <br>
                            A programmer is basically a problem-solver irrespective of the programming language used to solve the problem. <br>
                            Yes, we are working with C#. 
                            However, if you find out that Java would be better in solving a particular problem in your workplace, 
                            then you need to use Java unless your employer says otherwise. <br>
                            The knowledge of several languages is a good asset. It makes you highly valuable. <br>
                            Further, anyone who attempts any of the subsequent questions will still have to write the program in C# and get the same 
                            result. <br>
                            The questions and solutions are in another language. <br>
                            But the student will have to write the program in C# and come up with the same solution. So, we are still on track. <br>
                            It is critical thinking...multiple approaches to solving a question. <br>
                            Be it as it may, these subsequent questions are not required. <br>
                            It is okay even if you do not look at them. &#128522;

                    <h3>Some Substantive Responses</h3>
                    <p>
                        (1.) Review the post (Discuss Questions) done by your colleagues. <br>
                        Use a different statement to work on the <b>same Array</b> or the <b>same Tuple</b> used by your colleague. <br>
                        For example: if your colleague used an iteration statement in an array, you may use a conditional statement or a 
                        jump statement or a different iteration statement to write the <b>same Array</b> or the <b>same Tuple</b> to give the 
                        <b>same output.</b> <br><br>

                        (2.) Review the post (Discuss Questions) done by your colleagues. <br>
                        Use a different data structure to display the <b>same output</b> of the program done by your colleague. <br>
                        For example: if your colleague used an Array, use a Tuple to write the program to display the <b>same output.</b> <br><br>

                        (3.) Review the post (Discuss Questions and Assess Questions) done by your colleagues. <br>
                        If your colleague developed a console application for a program, develop a desktop application for the same program. <br> 
                        If your colleague developed a desktop application for a program, develop a console application for the same program. <br><br>

                        (4.) Review the post (Discuss Questions) done by your colleagues. <br>
                        Use a different user-defined array method and/or system-defined array class method to work on the <b>same program</b> 
                        done by your colleague. <br><br>

                        (5.) Other substantive responses.

                <h2>Module 1: Object-oriented Programming</h2>
        
                <div class="w3-row w3-center w3-border w3-xxlarge">
                    <a href="javascript:void(0)" onclick="openMenu10(event, 'Learn10');" id="myLink10">
                        <div class="w3-col s4 tablink10 w3-padding-large white w3-hover-language">Learn</div>
                    </a>
                    <a href="javascript:void(0)" onclick="openMenu10(event, 'Discuss10');">
                        <div class="w3-col s4 tablink10 w3-padding-large white w3-hover-language">Discuss</div>
                    </a>
                    <a href="javascript:void(0)" onclick="openMenu10(event, 'Assess10');">
                        <div class="w3-col s4 tablink10 w3-padding-large white w3-hover-language">Assess</div>
                    </a>
                </div>

                <div id="Learn10" class="w3-container menu10 w3-padding white">
                    <h3>Objectives</h3>
                    <p>
                        Students will: <br>
                        (1.) Discuss the terms used in object-oriented programming. <br>
                        (2.) Create a class. <br>
                        (3.) Create a constructor. <br>
                        (4.) Implement different constructors. <br>
                        (5.) Overload constructors. <br>
                        (6.) Create a destructor (destroy a constructor).
                    </p>
                
                    <h3>Vocabulary Words</h3>
                    <p>
                        object-oriented programming, class, object, namespace, access modifier, public, private, protected, internal,
                        protected internal, private protected, member access expression, field, attribute, property, assessor, method,
                        function, constructor, parametized constructor, copy constructor, default constructor, static constructor,
                        private constructor, overloading constructors, chaining constructors, this, keyword, concrete class,
                        enum, enumeration
                    </p>
                
                    <h3>Recommended Readings and Videos</h3>
                    <p>
                        (1.) Textbook Chapter <br><br>
                        (2.) Microsoft Tutorials: <a href="https://dotnet.microsoft.com/learn/csharp" target="_blank">Learn C#: Free Tutorials and Videos</a> <br>
                        (https://dotnet.microsoft.com/learn/csharp) <br><br>
                        (3.) Microsoft Tutorials: <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/tutorials/intro-to-csharp/introduction-to-classes" target="_blank">Classes and Objects</a> <br>
                        (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/tutorials/intro-to-csharp/introduction-to-classes) <br><br>
                        (4.) Microsoft Tutorials: <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/programming-guide/classes-and-structs/classes" target="_blank">Classes</a> <br>
                        (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/programming-guide/classes-and-structs/classes) <br><br>
                        (5.) Tutlane Tutorials: <a href="https://www.tutlane.com/tutorial/csharp/csharp-classes-and-objects-with-examples" target="_blank">Objects and Classes in C#:</a> <br>
                        (https://www.tutlane.com/tutorial/csharp/csharp-classes-and-objects-with-examples) <br>
                        Classes and Objects <b>to</b> C# Destructor <br><br>

                        <b>Videos:</b> <br>
                        Please click the <b>RESOURCES</b> tab of this website <br>
                        Click the <b>LinkedIn Learning (Kent State University)</b> link <br>
                        Follow the directions to access the website. <br>
                        (6.) Type <b>Object-oriented Programming in C#</b> and/or <b>OOP in C#</b> in the search bar and view the results. <br><br>
                        <b>Videos and Textbooks:</b> <br>
                        Please click the <b>RESOURCES</b> tab of this website <br>
                        Click the <b>O'Reilly Online Learning</b> link <br>
                        Follow the directions to access the website. <br>
                        (7.) Type <b>Object-oriented Programming in C#</b> and/or <b>OOP in C#</b> in the search bar and view/read the results.
                    </p>

                    <h3>Required Readings</h3>
                    <h4>Object-oriented Programming</h4>
                    <p>
                        <i>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> So, we discussed <b>data types and variables</b> <br>
                            We did some work/calculations with the data types and variables. <br>
                            Our project started to grow. <br>
                            We needed to lessen codes and reuse codes. Simply put, we wanted to do more with less. <br>
                            We introduced <b>functions</b> to achieve that goal. <br>
                            <b>Functions</b> included the <b>data types</b>, <b>variables</b> and the <b>work/calculations</b> done with
                            them. <br>
                            The project started getting large and popular. It needed to serve more clients. <br>
                            What should we do? <br>
                            Can we join <b>data types, variables</b> and <b>functions</b> as a package? Can we reuse that package as
                            much as we want? <br>
                            The answer is Yes! <br>
                            Welcome to <b>Classes</b> <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Where did the <b>object-oriented</b> come from? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Good question. <br>
                            Remember how we call a function any time we want to use that function? <br>
                            We shall 'call the class', well create a new instance of the class anytime we want to use that class. <br>
                            So, whenever we create an instance of the class because we want to use that class, we have created an
                            <b>object</b> <br>
                            That means that an <b>object</b> is an <b>instance of the class.</b> <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> So, what you are saying is that an object is simply calling the class in order to use it?
                            <br>
                            Just like we do in functions? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> That is correct. <br>
                            Simply put, <b>creating an object</b> means <b>calling the class.</b> <br>
                            We can create as many objects as we want by calling a class anytime we want. <br>
                            The class is a package of data types, variables and functions. <br>
                            Actually, a <b>class</b> is a package of <b>fields, methods</b>, and <b>properties</b>. <br>
                            <b>Creating and using objects</b> to develop computer programs is known as <b>object-oriented programming.</b>
                            <br>
                            <b>Encapsulation</b> is the combination of fields, properties, methods, and events of an object
                            in a class(capsule). <br>
                            In other words, keep all the data and program logic in one place. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> You introduced some words. <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Yes, I did. <br>
                            No worries you will get used to them. <br>
                            The <b>field</b> is the <b>data type</b> and <b>variable.</b> <br>
                            The <b>field</b> is often referred to as an <b>attribute.</b> <br>
                            The <b>method</b> is a <b>function</b> <br>
                            The <b>property</b> allows us to change the <b>fields.</b> <br>
                            The <b>fields, properties</b>, and <b>methods</b> are referred to as the <b>class members</b>
                        </i> <br><br>
                    
                        Recall: <br>
                        The first C# application we developed
                        <pre>
                            System.Console.WriteLine("THANK GOD");
                        </pre>
                    
                        <b>System</b> is the <b>namespace</b>
                        <b>Console</b> is a <b>class</b> in that <b>namespace</b> <br><br>
                    
                        We could write the same code this way:
                        
                        <pre>
                            using System;
                            Console.WriteLine("THANK GOD");
                        </pre>
                        
                        So, the <i>using</i> <b>keyword</b> for the <b>namespace</b>, <i>System</i> eliminates the need for writing
                        System.Console.WriteLine. <br>
                        This means that <b>Namespaces</b> are used to <b>organize classes.</b> <br><br>
                        
                        We shall also <b>declare</b> our own namespace using the <b>namespace</b> keyword. <br>
                        This allows us to control the scope of that class. <br>
                        The name of our &lt;namespace&gt; will use the Pascal casing, and will not be any of the reserved keywords. <br>
                        For large projects that involves several classes, using <b>namespaces</b> organizes those projects. <br>
                        <b>namespace</b> in <b>C#</b> is similar to a <b>package</b> in <b>Java</b> <br><br>
                        
                        In this example: <br>
                        Example 1: Explanation of a basic program <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule1CSharp/classes-example-1.png" alt="Classes: Example 1" style="width: 100%;"> <br><br>

                        We can write more classes under that namespace. <br><br>
                        The <b>class</b> (the <b>main class</b>) is <b>Program.</b> <br>
                        As you can see, it is also the name of the C# file, <b>Program.cs</b> that we created. <br>
                        This implies that the name of our C# file is also the name of the main class. <br><br>
                        The <b>Main(string[] args)</b> is the <b>method.</b> <br>
                        It is the <b>main method</b>. It has the name, <b>Main</b> <br><br>
                        The <b>void</b> is the <b>data type.</b> <br>
                        It means that it does not return any value. <br><br>
                        Looking at the hierachy on the RHS (Right Hand Side); <br>
                        we have the: <br>
                        (1.) Namespace <br>
                        (2.) Dependencies (do not worry about this for now). Notice I did not expand it. <br>
                        (3.) File name <br>
                        (4.) Class <br>
                        (5.) Method <br><br>
                        
                        <b>Class names</b> are written in <b>uppercase</b> or <b>Pascal case.</b> <br><br>

                    <h4>Access Modifiers</h4>
                    <p>
                        An <b>Access modifier</b> is a <b>keyword</b> used to <b>declare the accessibility of a class member or
                            type.</b>
                        <br>
                        It is used to control the visibility and security level of a class or class member. <br>
                        The first name of a person can be made public. However, the person's SSN (social security number) should not be
                        made public. <br>
                        A person's social security number should shoud of course, be made private. <br>
                        Similarly, there are some class members that can be made public and there are some that should not be
                        made public. <br><br>
                        In C#, we have <b>six access modifiers.</b> <br>
                        They are:
                    </p>
                
                        <table>
                            <caption>Access Modifiers and Accessibility Levels</caption>
                            <tr>
                                <th class="green w3-padding">Access Modifier</th>
                                <th class="green w3-padding">Accessibility Level</th>
                            </tr>
                            <tr>
                                <td class="w3-padding">public</td>
                                <td class="w3-padding">Access is not restricted. <br>
                                    The class member can be assessed outside the class that it was created.
                                </td>
                            </tr>
                            <tr>
                                <td class="w3-padding">private</td>
                                <td class="w3-padding">Access is limited to the containing type. <br>
                                    The class member can only be assessed within the class it was created.
                                </td>
                            </tr>
                            <tr>
                                <td class="w3-padding">protected</td>
                                <td class="w3-padding">Access is limited to the containing class or types derived from the containing
                                    class. <br>
                                    The class member can be assessed within the class it was created, or in a class that is
                                    <b>inherited</b> from the class that it was created.</td>
                            </tr>
                            <tr>
                                <td class="w3-padding">internal</td>
                                <td class="w3-padding">Access is limited to the current assembly.</td>
                            </tr>
                            <tr>
                                <td class="w3-padding">protected internal</td>
                                <td class="w3-padding">Access is limited to the current assembly or types derived from the containing
                                    class.</td>
                            </tr>
                            <tr>
                                <td class="w3-padding">private protected</td>
                                <td class="w3-padding">Access is limited to the containing class or types derived from the containing
                                    class within the current assembly.</td>
                            </tr>
                        </table>
                
                        <p>
                            <b>NOTE:</b> <br>
                            (1.) If an access modifier is not specified for a class member, that class member is private by default.
                            <br>
                            (2.) Access modifiers are not used with namespaces. Namespaces are not <i>actually</i> classes or class
                            members. They are used to organize classes. <br><br>
                
                            <i>
                                <b>Student:</b> What if students want to greet their fellow students? <br>

                                Let us name that file, ComputerScience.cs <br>
                                So, we will have two files: ComputerScience.cs (that will contain our class) and Program.cs (that
                                contains the main class). <br>
                                Keep in mind we already created this class. <br>
                                We just want to put it in another file so it is separate from the main class. <br>
                                Then, we can add as many members as we want.
                            </i> <br><br>
