                    Next, we need to demonstrate Multilevel Inheritance. <br>
                    Let us demonstrate it with <b>Geometry</b> <br><br>
                    
                    A <b>Quadrilateral</b> is a four-sided polygon. <br>
                    Examples include: <b>Square</b>, <b>Rectangle</b>, <b>Rhombus</b>, <b>Parallelogram</b>, <b>Kite</b>, and
                    <b>Trapezoid</b> <br>
                    Properties of a Quadrilateral are: <br>
                    (1.) It has four sides. <br>
                    (2.) It has four vertices. <br>
                    (3.) The sum of the interior angles of a quadrilateral is $360^\circ$ <br><br>
                    
                    A <b>Parallelogram</b> is a <b>quadrilateral</b> that has these properties: <br>
                    (1.) Opposite sides are equal. <br>
                    (2.) Opposite sides are parallel. <br>
                    (3.) Opposite interior angles are equal. <br><br>
                    
                    A <b>Square</b> is a <b>quadrilateral</b> that has these properties: <br>
                    (1.) All four sides are equal. <br>
                    (2.) Opposite sides are parallel. <br>
                    (3.) All angles are equal. <br><br>
                    
                    So, as we can see: a <b>Square</b> is also a <b>Parallelogram</b>. <br>
                    All squares are parallelograms but not all parallelograms are squares. <br>
                    Similarly, all parallelograms are quadrilaterals but not all quadrilaterals are parallelograms. <br>
                    That also implies that all squares are quadrilaterals but not all quadrilaterals are squares. <br><br>
                    In this context, let us take: <br>
                    <b>Quadrilateral</b> as the <b>parent</b> <br>
                    <b>Parallelogram</b> as the <b>child</b> <br>
                    <b>Square</b> as the <b>grandchild</b> <br><br>
                    
                    Please review the screenshots and the comments. <br><br>
                    <b>Multilevel Inheritance</b> <br>
                    <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/multilevel-inheritance-1.png" alt="Multilevel Inheritance 1"> <br>
                    <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/multilevel-inheritance-2.png" alt="Multilevel Inheritance 2"> <br>
                    <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/multilevel-inheritance-3.png" alt="Multilevel Inheritance 3"> <br>

                    </p>

                    <h4>Advantages of Inheritance</h4>
                    <p>
                        Let us discuss some advantages of Inheritance. <br>
                        (1.) <b>Code Reusability</b>: As seen in the previous examples, we reused the class fields of the base class. <br>
                        Inheritance is like the <b>DRY (Don't Repeat Yourself)</b> of programming because some class members of the base
                        class are reused. <br>
                        Reusability leads to reduced development and maintenance costs. <br><br>
                        (2.) <b>Code Extensibility</b>: Inheritance allows for modification and extension of code. <br>
                        As seen in the previous examples, we modified and extended the fields that was inherited from the base
                        class, in the derived class. <br><br>
                        (3.) <b>Creation of Class Libraries</b>: Inheritance facilitates the creation of class libraries. <br><br>
                        (4.) <b>Access to <i>protected</i> class members of the base class</b>: <br>
                        So far, we have: <br>
                        (a.) Used the <b>public</b> access specifier. <br>
                        (b.) Used the <b>private</b> access specifier. <br>
                        Recall that we had to use <b>Property</b> to access a <b>private class field</b>. <br>
                        But, what about the other access specifier: <b>protected</b>. We have not used it. <br>
                        We shall use it in this module. <br>
                        Recall that in the: <b>protected</b> access specifier: Access is limited to the containing class or types
                        derived from the containing class.
                        The class member can be assessed within the class it was created, or in a class that is <b>inherited</b>
                        from the class that it was created. <br>
                        The <b>protected</b> class members are similar to <b>private</b> class members. However, they can be
                        inherited. <br>
                        So, we shall be using the <b>protected</b> access specifier for any class member that we want to
                        encapsulate.
                        Please note that: <b>we still need to use Properties to access protected class members because technically,
                        they are still 'private'.</b> <br>
                        The only difference is that they can be inherited by derived class while private class members cannot be
                        inherited. <br>
                        So, they will be inherited. But, we shall use Properties (setter and getter) to access them. <br><br>
                        Let us demonstate the use of the <b>protected</b> access specifier. <br>
                        Let us do some math calculations. <br>
                    
                        <b>Simple Interest and Amount Calculations</b> <br>
                        Please review Question (5.) and the solution on Solved Examples on 
                        <a href="https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/financial-mathematics/simpleInterestApplications.html" target="_blank">Simple Interest website</a>: <br>
                        (https://samuelchukwuemeka.github.io/financial-mathematics/simpleInterestApplications.html) <br>
                        Let us calculate the simple interest and amount for several customers. <br>
                        We shall use Inheritance to solve that Question for several students. <br>
                        We shall make the interest rate of the financial institution and the time to be public, but make the first
                        names of the customers and the principal (sum of money deposited) to be protected. <br>
                        Please review the screenshots and the comments. <br><br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/inheritance-example-1-1.png" alt="Inheritance Example 1-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/inheritance-example-1-2.png" alt="Inheritance Example 1-2"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/inheritance-example-1-3.png" alt="Inheritance Example 1-3"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/inheritance-example-1-4.png" alt="Inheritance Example 1-4"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/inheritance-example-1-5.png" alt="Inheritance Example 1-5">                   
                    </p>
                    <h4>Disadvantages of Inheritance</h4>
                    <p>
                        (1.) <b>Coupling of the base class and the derived class:</b> The derived class is dependent on the base class to 
                        the degree that any change in the base class affects the derived class. <br>
                        Imagine if one deletes a class member of a base class that is also used in the derived class. <br>
                        For instance, in our demonstration of Multilevel Inheritance; if one of the properties of the Quadrilateral is 
                        deleted, the program will not run. <br><br>
                        (2.) <b>Execution time (Run time):</b> It takes more time for an inherited method to execute when compared to a 
                        method that did not use inheritance. <br>
                        One can write a program that uses a class to calculate the simple interest and amount of an investment without using 
                        inheritance. <br><br> 
                        (3.) Depending on the compiler, <b>unused base class members is a waste of memory:</b> When a derived class inherits
                        a base class, it may or may not use all the inherited class members of the base class. <br>
                        In the event that any base class member is not used in the derived class, it leads to a waste of resource/memory 
                        allocated to that class member. This typically depends on the compiler. <br>
                        Notice that in our examples, we did not use all the fields of the base class. 
                    </p>
                    
                    <h4>Same names for the Base Class Method and the Derived Class Method</h4>
                    <h4>New <i>keyword</i>, Virtual <i>keyword</i> and the Override <i>keyword</i></h4>
                    <p>
                        So far, we demonstrated the inheritance of the class fields of the base class. <br>
                        But remember: the methods of the base class can also be inherited. <br>
                        We discussed the <b>Second Approach to Inheritance</b> <br>
                        However, in that approach; the base class method and the derived class method had different names:
                        <b>BaseClassDetails()</b> and <b>DerivedClassDetails()</b>. <br><br>
                        What if they have the same name <u>and</u> we want different outputs for both methods, what should we do? <br>
                        When the base class and the derived class have the same method name, the base class method overrides the derived
                        class method. <br>
                        When the derived class inherits the method of the base class: <b>and</b> wants to modify the output/return value of
                        that method using the same name: <br>
                        We can do it in any of these two ways: <br><br>
                        (1.) Include the <b>new</b> keyword to the derived class method. <br>
                        Then, create two objects: one object of the base class to call the base class method <b>and</b> and another object
                        of the derived class to call the derived class method. <br><br>
                    
                        (2.) <b>Declare</b> the <b>method in the base class</b> with the <b>virtual</b> keyword <b>and</b> <b>declare</b>
                        the <b>method in the derived class</b> with the <b>override</b> keyword. <br>
                        Then, create two objects: one object of the base class to call the base class method <b>and</b> and another object
                        of the derived class to call the derived class method. <br><br>
                    
                        By doing so, we can run the methods in both the base class and the derived class. <br>
                        An example is the event that occurred in <b>July 2020</b> when <a href="https://www.cbsnews.com/news/georgia-governor-brian-kemp-bans-city-face-mask-orders-coronavirus-pandemic/" target="_blank">Governor Brian Kemp of the U.S State of Georgia banned cities and counties from enforcing a mask mandate</a>. <br>
                        (www.cbsnews.com/news/georgia-governor-brian-kemp-bans-city-face-mask-orders-coronavirus-pandemic/) <br>
                        In essence, the governor's executive order of not requiring masks overrides any law/ordinance by cities and counties
                        requiring masks. <br>
                        Let us review some code examples. Please review the screenshots and the comments. <br>
                        Let us assume (and of course the assumption is proper) that the City of Atlanta inherits from the State of Georgia.
                        <br>
                        Please review the screenshots and the comments. <br><br>
                        <b>Same method name: <i>new</i> keyword in the derived class method</b> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/new-keyword-1-1.png" alt="New keyword Example 1-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/new-keyword-1-2.png" alt="New keyword Example 1-2"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/new-keyword-1-3.png" alt="New keyword Example 1-3"> <br><br>
                    
                        <b>Same method name: <i>virtual</i> keyword in the base class method and <i>override</i> keyword in the derived class method</b> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/virtual-override-keyword-1-1.png" alt="Virtual-Override keyword Example 1-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/virtual-override-keyword-1-2.png" alt="Virtual-Override keyword Example 1-2"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/virtual-override-keyword-1-3.png" alt="Virtual-Override keyword Example 1-3">
                    </p>
                    
                    <h4>Sealed <i>keyword</i></h4>
                    <p>
                        The <b>sealed</b> keyword is used in any base class that does not want any derived class to inherit from it. <br>
                        In other words, no class can inherit anything from a base class that is declared with the <i>sealed</i> keyword.
                        <br>
                        A general belief is that the decision of the Supreme Court is sealed. Is that right, people? (<i>But, does the
                            Supreme Court have the last word? I think Christians would disagree.</i>) <br>
                        No other court will hear any case decided by the Supreme Court. <br>
                        Most importantly (Bring it to History as this is an election year): the <a href="https://www.history.com/news/2000-election-bush-gore-votes-supreme-court" target="_blank">2000 Presidential Election</a> <br>
                        (www.history.com/news/2000-election-bush-gore-votes-supreme-court) was <b>sealed</b> by the Supreme Court.
                        <br><br>
                        Let us look at a code example. Please review the screenshots and the comments. <br><br>
                        <b><i>sealed</i> keyword</b> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/sealed-keyword-1-1.png" alt="Sealed keyword Example 1-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/sealed-keyword-1-2.png" alt="Sealed keyword Example 1-2">  <br><br>
                        Because a sealed class cannot be inherited, it cannot be a base class. <br>
                        Because it cannot be a base class, it cannot be an abstract class. No worries, we shall deal with abstract classes in the next section. <br>
                        Because it cannot be used as a base class, calling sealed class members can be made faster by some run-time optimizations. 
                    </p>
                    
                    <h3>Abstraction</h3>
                    <p>
                        <b>Abstraction</b> is the process of hiding sensitive data while exposing only the required properties and methods.
                        <br><br>
                        <i>
                            Compare it to a situation where we have a private class field, but use a public property to access that field
                            (the Get and Set accessors).
                        </i> <br><br>
                        Abstraction is done using: <br>
                        (1.) <b>Abstract class</b> <br>
                        (2.) <b>Interface</b>                    
                    </p>

                    <h4>Abstract Class</h4>
                    <p>
                        An <b>abstract class</b> is a class that: <br><br>
                        (1.) Is declared using the <b>abstract</b> keyword. <br><br>
                        (2.) Cannot be used to create objects. <br>
                        In other words, it cannot be instantiated directly using the <b>new</b> operator. <br>
                        Because it cannot be used to create objects, it cannot have a constructor. <br><br>
                        (3.) Can be used as a base class or a derived class. Most times, it is used as a base class. <br>
                        It must be inherited by another class. <br>
                        But, it can also inherit a class. <br>
                        When an abstract class inherits a virtual method from a base class, the abstract class can override the 
                        virtual method with an abstract method. <br><br>
                        (4.) May contain <b>abstract members (including abstract methods).</b> <br>
                        If it contains an <b>abstract member</b>, that member must be declared with the <b>abstract</b> keyword. <br><br>
                        (5.) May contain <b>non-abstract</b> members. <br><br>
                        The reason for using an abstract class is to provide a common definition of a base class that multiple derived classes 
                        can share. <br>
                        For example: an abstract class can be used as a parameter to several functions of a class library, and require 
                        programmers using that library to implement the class by creating a derived class. 
                    </p>

                    <h4>Abstract Method</h4>
                    <p>
                        An <b>Abstract method</b> is a method that: <br>
                        (1.) Is declared using the <b>abstract</b> keyword. <br><br>
                        (2.) Have no implementation. <br>
                        It does not have a body. <br>
                        Hence, it is followed by a semicolon rather than the normal method block. <br>
                        The *implementation* of an abstract method is provided by the derived class that inherits it. <br>
                        Note that the method of the derived class must <b>override</b> that abstract method in order to implement it's 
                        own method (because the abstract method does not have any implementation). <br><br>
                        (3.) Must be used only in an abstract class. 
                     </p>

                     <h4>Concrete Class (Non-Abstract Class)</h4>
                     <p>
                        A <b>concrete class</b> is a nonabstract class that can instantiate objects. <br>
                        In other words, it can be instantiated directly using the <b>new</b> operator. 
                     </p>

                    <h4>Interface</h4>
                    <p>
                        <i>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Remember we discussed earlier that C# does not support multiple inheritances. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Yes. <br>
                            C# does not support multiple inheritances. But C++ does. <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> That is correct. <br>
                            However, C# supports multiple interfaces. <br>
                            So, if you want to demonstrate multiple inheritances with C#, you can do so with multiple interfaces as 
                            the parents.
                        </i> <br><br>
                        An <b>Interface</b> is <b><i>like</i></b> an abstract base class that: <br>
                        (1.) Is declared using the <b>interface</b> keyword. <br><br>
                        (2.) Is named using a valid C# identifier and which by convention, must begin with an uppercase I. <br><br>
                        (3.) Can contain instance methods, properties, events, indexers, or any combination of these member types. <br><br>
                        (4.) Cannot contain instance fields, instance constructors, or finalizers. <br>
                        However, it may contain static constructors, static fields, constants, or operators. <br><br>
                        (5.) Contains definitions for a group of related functionalities that a non-abstract class or struct must implement. <br>
                        <i>Struct</i> is not covered in this course. <br><br>
                        (6.) Is used to simulate inheritance for structs because structs cannot inherit from another struct or class. <br> 
                        <i>Struct</i> is not covered in this course. <br><br>
                        (7.) Is inherited using the colon :  <br>
                        Multiple interfaces can be inherited. <br>
                        C# supports the inheritance of multiple interfaces, but not the inheritance of multiple classes. 
                    </p>
                    <h5>Properties of Interfaces</h5>
                    <p>
                        (1.) An interface contains <b>only abstract public members.</b> <br>
                        Any class that implements an interface must implement all the members of that interface. <br>
                        An interface may define default implementations for some or all of its members. <br><br>
                        (2.) An interface cannot be used to create objects. <br>
                        In other words, it cannot be instantiated directly using the <b>new</b> operator. <br>
                        The members of an interface are implemented by any class ot struct that implements the interface. <br>
                        To implement any member of an interface, the corresponding member of the implementing class must be public, 
                        non-static, and have the same name and signature as that interface member. <br><br>
                        (3.) A class or struct can implement multiple interfaces. <br>
                        Further, a class (not a struct) can inherit a base class and also implement multiple interfaces. <br><br>

                        <i>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Let us demonstrate the inheritance of multiple interfaces. <br>
                            We shall use the same example that we used when we demonstrated <b>Multiple Inheritance in C++</b> <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> You mean the Zoology example for the dog breeds? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Yes. 
                        </i> <br><br>
                        <b>Multiple Interfaces</b> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/interface-1-1.png" alt="Multiple Interface 1-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/interface-1-2.png" alt="Multiple Interface 1-2"> <br>

                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/interface-1-7.png" alt="Multiple Interface 1-7">
                    </p>
                    
                    <h3>Polymorphism</h3>
                    <p>
                        Literally: <i>poly</i> means <i>many</i> and <i>morphs</i> means <i>forms</i>; so <i>polymorphism</i> means 
                        <i>many forms</i> <br> 
                        <b>Polymorphism</b> is the ability of classes to: <br>
                        (I.) Invoke the methods of a derived class by referencing a base class at <b>run time</b> and <br>
                        (II.) Implement different methods called by the same name at <b>compile time</b> <br><br>
                        There are basically two kinds of polymorphism: <br>
                        (1.) Dynamic Polymorphism <br>
                        (2.) Static Polymorphism 
                    </p>

                    <h4>Dynamic Polymorphism (Run-time Polymorphism or Late-binding Polymorphism)</h4>
                    <p>
                        This polymorphism: <br><br>
                        (1.) Occurs at <b>run time</b> <br><br>
                        (2.) Also referred to as <b>late binding polymorphism</b> because the decision regarding the method to be called
                        is made at run time. <br><br>
                        (3.) <b>Overrides</b> the method of a base class by using the same name and parameters of that method in a
                        derived class in order to perform a different task. (<b>Method Overriding</b>) <br>
                        For this to occur, the <b>method in the base class</b> must use the <b>virtual</b> keyword while the
                        <b>same method in the derived class</b> must use the <b>override</b> keyword. <br><br>
                        Let us demonstrate Dynamic Polymorphism <br>
                        Back to Geometry: Quadrilaterals <br>
                        A <b>Quadrilateral</b> is a four-sided polygon. <br>
                        These figures are <b>Quadrilaterals</b>. <br>
                        (1.) Square <br>
                        (2.) Rhombus <br>
                        (3.) Rectangle <br>
                        (4.) Parallelogram <br>
                        (5.) Kite <br>
                        (6.) Trapezoid <br><br>
                        We shall calculate the perimeter of these quadrilaterals. <br>
                        The formulas for calculating the perimeters of quadrilaterals are found here:
                        <a href="https://www.samuelchukwuemeka.com/Mensuration/formulas.html" target="_blank">Mensuration Formulas</a> <br>
                        (www.samuelchukwuemeka.com/Mensuration/formulas.html) <br>
                        The website also contains the calculators for calculating perimeters. <br>
                        You may test the output of this program with the <a href="https://www.samuelchukwuemeka.com/Mensuration/mensuration.html" target="_blank">Mensuration Calculators</a> 
                        on the website. <br><br>
                        We shall list the parameters used for calculating the perimeters of the quadrilaterals. <br>
                        The parameters are the class fields of the base class: Quadrilateral.cs <br>
                        Main Class: <b>Program.cs</b> <br>
                        Base Class: <b>Quadrilateral.cs</b> <br>
                        Child Class: <b>Square.cs</b> <br>
                        Child Class: <b>Rectangle.cs</b> <br>
                        Child Class: <b>Rhombus.cs</b> <br>
                        Child Class: <b>Parallelogram.cs</b> <br>
                        Child Class: <b>Trapezoid.cs</b> <br>
                        Child Class: <b>Kite.cs</b> <br><br>
                        
                        To calculate the perimeter of a: <br>
                        (I.) Square and Rhombus: only a side is needed. <br>
                        So, we shall define <b>side</b> as one of the class fields of the Quadrilateral <br><br>
                        (II.) Rectangle and Parallelogram: the length and the width is needed. <br>
                        So, we shall include <b>length</b> and <b>width</b> as class fields of the Quadrilateral <br><br>
                        (III.) Kite: A kite has two pairs of equal sides. <br>
                        We shall define them as <b>side1Kite</b> and <b>side2kite</b> and include them as class fields of the Quadrilateral
                        <br><br>
                        (IV.) Trapezoid: A trapezoid has no equal side. <br>
                        The Quadrilateral class fields for these sides are: <b>side1Trapezoid</b>, <b>side2Trapezoid</b>, <b>side3Trapezoid</b>,
                        and <b>side4Trapezoid</b> <br><br>
                        The other class fields we need for the Quadrilateral.cs class are the fields that define the perimeters for each
                        quadrilateral. <br>
                        Let us demonstrate <b>Dynamic Polymorphism (Polymorphism at Run-time)</b> <br>
                        Please review the screenshots and the comments. <br><br>
                        
                        <b>Dynamic Polymorphism (Polymorphism at Run-time)</b> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/dynamic-polymorphism-1-1.png" alt="Dynamic Polymorphism 1-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/dynamic-polymorphism-1-2.png" alt="Dynamic Polymorphism 1-2"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/dynamic-polymorphism-1-3.png" alt="Dynamic Polymorphism 1-3"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/dynamic-polymorphism-1-4.png" alt="Dynamic Polymorphism 1-4"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/dynamic-polymorphism-1-5.png" alt="Dynamic Polymorphism 1-5"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/dynamic-polymorphism-1-6.png" alt="Dynamic Polymorphism 1-6"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/dynamic-polymorphism-1-7.png" alt="Dynamic Polymorphism 1-7"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/dynamic-polymorphism-1-8.png" alt="Dynamic Polymorphism 1-8"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/dynamic-polymorphism-1-9.png" alt="Dynamic Polymorphism 1-9"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/dynamic-polymorphism-1-10.png" alt="Dynamic Polymorphism 1-10"> <br>

                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/dynamic-polymorphism-1-14.png" alt="Dynamic Polymorphism 1-14"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/dynamic-polymorphism-1-15.png" alt="Dynamic Polymorphism 1-15"> 
                    </p>

                    <h4>Static Polymorphism (Compile-time Polymorphism or Early-binding Polymorphism)</h4>
                    <p>
                        This polymorphism: <br><br>
                        (1.) Occurs at <b>compile time</b> <br><br>
                        (2.) Also referred to as <b>early binding polymorphism</b> because the decision regarding the method to be called
                        is made at compile time. <br><br>
                        (3.) <b>Overloads</b> the method of a class by using the same name but different number and/or parameter types of
                        that method in a
                        class in order to perform different tasks. (<b>Method Overloading</b>) <br><br>
                        For this to occur, the <b>method in the class must not have the same number <u>and</u> parameter type to perform
                            those different tasks</b>. <br><br>
                        (4.) <b>Overloads</b> the operator of a class to perform different tasks. (<b>Operator Overloading</b>) <br>
                        The syntax for Operator Overloading is:
                        <pre>
                            {
                                public static className operator *operator* (parameters)
                                ...create object
                                ...write code
                                return object
                            }
                        </pre>
                        *operator* is the operator to be overloaded. <br>
                        Static Polymorphism consists of Method Overloading and Operator Overloading. <br>
                        Let us demonstrate Method Overloading in Static Polymorphism. <br>
                        Let us just focus on Rectangle. <br>
                        Another property we shall add is that each angle in a Rectangle is a right angle. (an angle of 90 degrees). <br>
                        We shall overload the methods with different parameter data types: (integer data type to calculate the sum of the
                        interior angles in a Rectangle; string data type to list the properties of a Rectangle; and two double data types to
                        calculate the perimeter of a Rectangle). <br>
                        We can also have a parameterless method to list the properties of a Rectangle rather than using the string data type
                        as a parameter. <br>
                        Please review the screenshots and the comments. <br><br>
                        <b>Static Polymorphism: Method Overloading</b> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/static-polymorphism-method-overloading-1-1.png" alt="Static Polymorphism: Method Overloading 1-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/static-polymorphism-method-overloading-1-2.png" alt="Static Polymorphism: Method Overloading 1-2"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/static-polymorphism-method-overloading-1-3.png" alt="Static Polymorphism: Method Overloading 1-3"> <br><br>

                        Let us demonstrate Operator Overloading of the Addition Operator, $+$ in Static Polymorphism. <br>
                        So, we need objects that will be added. <br>
                        Still working with Rectangles, let us calculate the sum of the Perimeters of Two Rectangles. <br>
                        The formula for calculating the perimeter of a rectangle is found here:
                        <a href="https://www.samuelchukwuemeka.com/Mensuration/formulas.html" target="_blank">Mensuration Formulas</a> <br>
                        (https://www.samuelchukwuemeka.com/Mensuration/formulas.html) <br>
                        The website also contains the calculators for calculating perimeters. <br>
                        You may test the output of this program with the <a href="https://www.samuelchukwuemeka.com/Mensuration/mensuration.html" target="_blank">Mensuration Calculators</a> 
                        on the website. <br><br>
                        Please review the screenshots and the comments. <br><br>
                        <b>Static Polymorphism: Operator Overloading</b> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/static-polymorphism-operator-overloading-1-1.png" alt="Static Polymorphism: Operator Overloading 1-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/static-polymorphism-operator-overloading-1-2.png" alt="Static Polymorphism: Operator Overloading 1-2"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/static-polymorphism-operator-overloading-1-3.png" alt="Static Polymorphism: Operator Overloading 1-3"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/static-polymorphism-operator-overloading-1-4.png" alt="Static Polymorphism: Operator Overloading 1-4"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule4CSharp/static-polymorphism-operator-overloading-1-5.png" alt="Static Polymorphism: Operator Overloading 1-5"> <br><br>

                        <i>
                            Quick Question  for you. <br>
                            Please choose the correct option.
                        </i> <br>
                        <b>
                            <i>
                                Which is the correct option for the explanation of the sum of the perimeters of the rectangles we
                                calculated using Addtion Operator Overloading? <br>
                                Give at least one reason for your answer. <br>
                            </i>
                        </b> <br>                        
                        (A.) $7$ unit is the length and $3$ unit is the width of the first rectangle. <br>
                        Using the formula in the method, we got $20$ unit as the perimeter of the first rectangle. <br>
                        $14$ unit is the length and $10$ unit is the width of the second rectangle. <br>
                        Using the same formula, we got $48$ unit as the perimeter of the second rectangle. <br>
                        Then, we overloaded the addtion operator to add the two perimeters in order to get the sum. <br>
                        The sum of the perimeters of the two rectangles is $68$ unit because it is the result of the addition of 
                        $20$ unit and $48$ unit. <br><br>
                        (B.) $7$ unit is the length of the first rectangle and $14$ unit is the length of the second rectangle. <br>
                        We overloaded the addtion operator to get the sum of the lengths as $21$ unit. <br>
                        $3$ unit is the width of the first rectangle and $10$ unit is the width of the second rectangle. <br>
                        We overloaded the addtion operator to get the sum of the widths as $13$ unit. <br>
                        Then, we used the formula in the method to get the perimeter. <br>
                        That perimeter is the sum. <br><br>
                        (C.) Options (A.) and (B.) are both correct. <br><br>
                        (D.) Does it really matter? The main thing is that the answer is correct. <br>
                        $48$ unit is the sum of the perimeters of the two rectangles. 
                    </p>
                    
                    <button class="solutions" onclick="solutions('solutionAC1')">Show/Hide Answer</button>
                    <div class="answer" id="solutionAC1">
                        <p>
                            The correct option is $B$ <br>
                            If you did not answer Option $B$, please review the screenshots and the comments again. <br>
                            Then, let me know if you have any questions.
                        </p>
                    </div>

                    <h3>Required Readings</h3>
                    <h3>Arrays as Objects</h3>
                    <p>
                        We discussed Arrays in the previous course: C# (Beginning C#) <br>
                        (1.) There, we learned that arrays are data structures for storing multiple variables of the same data type. <br><br>
                        (2.) We also learned that we declare arrays by specifying the type of its elements. <br><br>
                        (3.) We learned about single-dimensional arrays, multidomensional arrays, and jagged arrays. <br><br>
                        (4.) The number of dimensions and the length of each dimension are established when the array instance is created. <br>
                        Once the array instance is created, the number of dimensions and the length of each dimension cannot be changed during the 
                        lieftime of the instance. <br><br>
                        (5.) Arrays are zero-indexed. The first element in the array is at index: $0$ and the last element is at index: $n-1$ 
                        where $n$ is the length or size of the array... among others. <br><br> 
                        We discussed Classes and Objects in this course: Advanced C# <br>
                        Here, we shall discuss Arrays of  Objects. <br>
                        (6.) Arrays are objects. Yes, we can implement them as objects. <br><br>
                        (7.) We can use array to store elements of different types (not only elements of the same type). <br>
                        To do so, we need to specify <b>object</b> as its type. <br><br>
                        (8.) The default values of numeric arrays elements are set to $0$, while the reference elements are set to <b>null</b> <br><br>
                        (9.) Array types are <b>reference types.</b> <br>
                        Variables of reference types store references to their objects. Two variables can reference the same object. Operations on one 
                        variable can affect the object referenced by the other variable. <br><br>
                        (10.) <b>Array</b> is the abstract base type through which all the array types are derived from. <br>
                        The Array type implements the <b>IEnumerable</b> Interface and the <b>IEnumerable&lt;T&gt;</b> Interface. <br>
                        Hence, the <b>foreach</b> loop can be used on all C# arrays. <br><br>
                        
                        We can use the properties and members of the Array class. <br>
                        To demonstrate arrays as objects, we simply declare them with the <b>new</b> keyword, specify the type, and initialize them. <br>
                        Please review the screenshots and the comments. <br><br>

                        <b>Array as Objects</b> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule5CSharp/arrayObject-1-1.png" alt="Array  1-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule5CSharp/arrayObject-1-2.png" alt="Array  1-2">
                    </p>

                    <h3>Array Class</h3>
                    <p>
                        The <b>Array</b> Class provides the methods for creating, manipulating, searching, 
                        and sorting arrays. <br>
                        It is the base class for all arrays in the common language runtime. <br>
                        Please review the implementation of the methods of the Array class. <br>
                        <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.array?view=netcore-3.1" target="_blank">Array Class</a> <br>
                        https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.array?view=netcore-3.1)
                    </p>
                    
                    <h3>Tuple Class</h3>
                    <p>
                        We discussed Tuples in the previous course: C# (Beginning C#) <br>
                        There, we learned that tuples: <br>
                        (1.) Are data structures for storing multiple variables of the same or different data types. <br><br>
                        (2.) Have a specific number and sequence of elements. <br><br>
                        (3.) Can be nested. <br><br>
                        (4.) Provides easy access to a dataset and manipulation of the dataset. <br><br>
                        (5.) Can return multiple values from a method without using the <b>out</b> parameters. <br><br>
                        (6.) Can pass multiple values to a method through a single parameter. <br><br>
                        (7.) Can be returned from a method using the <b>out</b> parameter...among others.  
                    </p>
                    <p>
                        In this class: Advanced C#; we learn that: <br>
                        The <b>Tuple class</b> is the class that provides <b>static methods</b> for creating <b>tuple objects.</b> <br>
                        It is <b>not</b> a tuple. It is the class that provides static methods for creating instances of the tuple 
                        types that are supported by the .NET framework. <br>
                        An object of the tuple class (an instance of the tuple class or tuple object) can be created in two ways: <br><br>
                        (1.) Use of a <b>helper method</b> provided by the tuple class. <br>
                        This approach uses the <b>Tuple.Create</b> method. <br>
                        It is a static method. <br>
                        The syntax is: 
                        <pre>
                            var objectName = Tuple.Create(value1, value2, value3, ...);
                        </pre>
                        (2.) Calling the tuple class <b>constructor.</b> <br>
                        This approach uses the <b>new Tuple</b> keyword. <br>
                        The syntax is:
                        <pre>
                            var objectName = new Tuple&lt;dataType1, dataType, dataType3, ...&gt;
                                (value1, value2, value3, ...)
                        </pre>
                        <i>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Which approach do you prefer? <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> The first one of course <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> I prefer that approach as well. <br>
                            We shall use that approach to demonstrate an application of the tuple class. <br>
                            But, let us show examples of both approaches before we move on to the application of the tuple class. 
                        </i>
                    </p>
                   
                    <p>
                        <b>Creating Tuple Objects</b> <br><br>
                        <b>First Approach: Helper Method</b> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule5CSharp/tupleObject-1.png" alt="Tuple Object 2"> <br><br>

                        <b>Second Approach: Class Constructor</b> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule5CSharp/tupleObject-2.png" alt="Tuple Object 2">
                    </p>

                        As you can see from the codes in the screenshots, we can change the values of any of those tuple elements 
                        if we want to. <br><br>

                        Please review the implementation of the examples and methods of all the Tuple classes. <br>
                        These include the: <br>
                        (1.) 1-tuple or Singleton: 
                        <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.tuple-1?view=netcore-3.1" target="_blank">Tuple&lt;T1&gt; Class</a> <br>
                        (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.tuple-1?view=netcore-3.1) <br><br>
                        (2.) 2-tuple or Pair: 
                        <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.tuple-2?view=netcore-3.1" target="_blank">Tuple&lt;T1, T2&gt; Class</a> <br>
                        (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.tuple-2?view=netcore-3.1) <br><br>
                        (3.) 3-tuple or Triple: 
                        <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.tuple-3?view=netcore-3.1" target="_blank">Tuple&lt;T1, T2, T3&gt; Class</a> <br>
                        (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.tuple-3?view=netcore-3.1) <br><br>
                        (4.) 4-tuple or Quadruple: 
                        <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.tuple-4?view=netcore-3.1" target="_blank">Tuple&lt;T1, T2, T3, T4&gt; Class</a> <br>
                        (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.tuple-4?view=netcore-3.1) <br><br>
                        (5.) 5-tuple or Quintuple: 
                        <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.tuple-5?view=netcore-3.1" target="_blank">Tuple&lt;T1, T2, T3, T4, T5&gt; Class</a> <br>
                        (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.tuple-5?view=netcore-3.1) <br><br>
                        (6.) 6-tuple or Sextuple: 
                        <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.tuple-6?view=netcore-3.1" target="_blank">Tuple&lt;T1, T2, T3, T4, T5, T6&gt; Class</a> <br>
                        (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.tuple-6?view=netcore-3.1) <br><br>
                        (7.) 7-tuple or Septuple: 
                        <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.tuple-7?view=netcore-3.1" target="_blank">Tuple&lt;T1, T2, T3, T4, T5, T6, T7&gt; Class</a> <br>
                        (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.tuple-7?view=netcore-3.1) <br><br>
                        (8.) 8-tuple or Octuple: 
                        <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.tuple-8?view=netcore-3.1" target="_blank">Tuple&lt;T1, T2, T3, T4, T5, T6, T7, T8&gt; Class</a> <br>
                        (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.tuple-8?view=netcore-3.1) <br><br>
                        where: <br>
                        T1 is the type of the first component of the tuple <br>
                        T2 is the type of the second component of the tuple <br>
                        T3 is the type of the third component of the tuple <br>
                        T4 is the type of the fourth component of the tuple <br>
                        T5 is the type of the fifth component of the tuple <br>
                        T6 is the type of the sixth component of the tuple <br>
                        T7 is the type of the seventh component of the tuple <br>
                        T8 is the type of the eighth component of the tuple <br>
                        TRest is any generic Tuple object that defines the types of the tuple's remaining components. <br><br>

                        <i>
                            <b>Student:</b> Is that all? <br>
                            Keep going...so much you want us to learn <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> No worries... you can stop here. <br>
                            The static helper method of the tuple class does not support the creation of tuple objects with more than 
                            eight components. <br>
                            Notice that in the example we did, the tuple object output was seven components. <br>
                            Try to keep it at seven components. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> What if we wanted more? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Use array of objects. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> But, what if I wanted to use tuple objects? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Then you have to use the tuple class constructor. <br>
                            But, that is cumbersome. <br>
                            To create an n-tuple with nine or more components, you must call the Tuple&lt;T1,T2,T3,T4,T5,T6,T7,TRest&gt; constructor.
                        </i>
                    </p>

                </div>

                    <h3>Vocabulary Words</h3>
                    <p>
                        file, stream, file and stream I/O, System.IO namespaces, storage, backing store, byte, directory path, 
                        disk storage, file name, directory name, file stream, File class, FileInfo class, FileStream class, 
                        TextReader class, TextWriter class, StringReader class, StringWriter class, StreamReader class, 
                        StreamWriter class, ReadAllText method, ReadAllLines method, WriteAllText method, WriteAllLines method,
                        AppendAllText method, AppendAllLines method, sequential access file, synchronous file operations, 
                        random access file processing, text file processing, object-based file processing, BinaryReader class,
                        BinaryWriter class
                    </p>
        
                    <h3>Objectives</h3>
                    <p>
                        Students will: <br><br>
                        (1.) Write programs that reads text from a text file. <br>
                        (2.) Write programs that writes text to a text file. <br>
                        (3.) Write programs that appends text to a text file. <br>
                        (4.) Write programs that reads characters from a string. <br>
                        (5.) Write programs that writes characters to a string. <br>
                        (6.) Implement the methods of the File class. <br>
                        (7.) Implement the methods of the FileInfo class. <br>
                        (8.) Implement the methods of the FileStream class.
                    </p>

                    <h3>Required Readings</h3>
                    <p>
                        A <b>file</b> is an <b>ordered and named collection of bytes</b> that has a storage medium. <br>
                        Working with files implies working with directory paths, disk storage, and file and directory names. <br><br>
                        
                        A <b>stream</b> is a sequence of bytes that is used to read from, and write to a storage medium. <br>
                        Streams involve three fundamental operations. <br>
                        These operations are: <br>
                        (1.) Reading: This involves the transfer of data from a stream into a data structure, such as an array of bytes. <br>
                        (2.) Writing: This involves the transfer of data to a stream from a data source. <br>
                        (3.) Seeking: This involves the querying and modifying of the current position within a stream. <br><br>

                        The storage medium is also known as backing store. <br>
                        These include disks and memory among others. <br><br>
                        <b>File and stream I/O (Input/Output)</b> is the transfer of data to, or from a storage medium. <br><br>
                        The <b>System.IO namespaces</b> in the .NET Framework contain types that enable reading and writing, both
                        synchronously and asynchronously, on files and streams. <br><br>
                        For Files: <br>
                        The System.IO namespace provide types that: <br>
                        (1.) Get and set properties for files. <br>
                        (2.) Retrieve collections of files based on search criteria. <br><br>
                    
                        For Streams: <br>
                        The System.IO namespace provide types that: <br>
                        (1.) Read encoded characters from streams. <br>
                        (2.) Write encoded characters to streams. <br><br>
                    
                        Under the System.IO namespace, there are several classes. <br>
                        In this module, we shall <u>only focus on these classes.</u> <br><br>
                        <b>File and Directory Classes</b> <br>
                        (1.) File Class (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.file?view=netframework-4.8) <br>
                        (2.) FileInfo Class (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.fileinfo?view=netcore-3.1) <br><br>
                    
                        <b>Stream Class</b> <br>
                        (3.) FileStream Class (https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.filestream?view=netframework-4.8)
                        <br><br>
                    
                        <b>Reader and Writer Classes</b> <br>
                        (4.) <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.textreader?view=netcore-3.1" target="_blank">TextReader Class</a> <br>
                        (www.docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.textreader?view=netcore-3.1) <br><br>
                        and <br><br>
                        <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.textwriter?view=netcore-3.1" target="_blank">TextWriter Class</a> <br>
                        (www.docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.textwriter?view=netcore-3.1) <br><br>

                        (5.) <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.stringreader?view=netcore-3.1" target="_blank">StringReader Class</a> <br>
                        (www.docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.stringreader?view=netcore-3.1) <br><br>
                        and <br><br>
                        <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.stringwriter?view=netcore-3.1" target="_blank">StringWriter Class</a> <br>
                        (www.docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.stringwriter?view=netcore-3.1) <br><br>
                        
                        (6.) <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.streamreader?view=netcore-3.1" target="_blank">StreamReader Class</a> <br>
                        (www.docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.streamreader?view=netcore-3.1) <br><br>
                        and <br><br>
                        <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.streamwriter?view=netcore-3.1" target="_blank">StreamWriter Class</a> <br>
                        (www.docs.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.streamwriter?view=netcore-3.1) <br><br>
                        
                        <b>Sequential access file processing</b> is the ability of the computer to read or write to the file sequentially
                        (from beginning to end).
                        These are common with text files. <br>
                        Compare <b>sequential access file processing</b> with <b>synchronous file operation</b>. <br>
                    
                        <b>Random access file processing</b> is the ability of the computer to read or write to the file anywhere (at any
                        position) in the file. These are common with database files. <br><br>
                        Be it as it may, the computer can read sequential files randomly, as well as read random files sequentially. <br>
                        We studied: <b>Strings, Booleans, Date and Time</b> and <b>Arrays</b> in the Beginning C#. <br>
                        We studied: <b>Array Class</b> and <b>Tuple Class</b> in the previous module in this Advanced C#. <br>
                        We have also reviewed several String methods/functions. <br>
                        We shall use some our prior knowledge from those topics, in this module. <br><br>
                        For this module, we are only concerned with <b>synchronous file operations</b> (read synchronously, write synchronously, append synchronously).
                    </p>

                    <h4>Example $1$: First Approach: Read from a text file using the StreamReader class</h4>
                    <p>
                        This approach has a limitation: the storage/location of your file. <br>
                        We shall use the <b>StreamReader</b> class to read a text file. <br>
                        It reads a text file one line at a time. However, we have to save our file in a particular location in order to read
                        it. <br>
                        Pick any topic in an academic area/field/discipline of your choice. <br>
                        I am going to write some notes about our next module: <b>XAML: Extensible Application Markup Language</b> <br>
                        But, you may choose whatever you want, provided it is in an academic discipline. <br><br>
                    
                        The steps are: <br>
                        (1.) Create a text file about the topic: say <b>Module7Notes.txt</b> <br><br>
                        (2.) Write some notes in the file. I shall write five sentences. <br>
                        This is what I wrote: <br>
                        <code>
                            (1.) XAML is an acronym which stands for eXtensible Markup Application Language. <br>
                            (2.) It is pronounced as "Zamel". <br>
                            (3.) It is a declarative markup language used for creating a user interface for a .NET Core app. <br>
                            (4.) The files are XML files but with .xaml extension. <br>
                            (5.) It was developed by Microsoft. <br>
                        </code> <br><br>
                        (3.) Open the Visual Studio IDE. <br><br>
                        (4.) Add the <b>System.IO</b> namespace by writing <b>using System.IO</b> <br><br>
                        (5.) <b>Important:</b> Please make sure you save your file where the IDE would easily access it. <br>
                        When you created the C# application, you named the application. <br>
                        I named my application: <b>FileStream</b> <br>
                        Go to the location of your application. My applications are in the Documents folder of my C: drive. <br>
                        Click the application folder: <b>FileStream</b> <br>
                        Then, click the <b>bin</b> folder, then the <b>Debug</b> folder, then the <b>netcoreapp3.1</b> folder, and drop your
                        text file <b>Module7Notes.txt</b> there. <br>

                            ---> Module7Notes.txt</i> <br>
                        The location is: <b>C:\Users\chukw\Documents\C#\FileStream\bin\Debug\netcoreapp3.1\Module7Notes.txt</b> <br><br>
                        (6.) Please review the screenshots and the comments. <br><br>

                    <h4>Example $2$: Second Approach: Read from a text file using the ReadAllText method of the System.IO.File class</h4>
                    <p>
                        I prefer this approach. <br>
                        It reads the contents of a text file by using the static methods: <b>ReadAllText</b> and <b>ReadAllLines</b> from
                        the <b>System.IO.File class</b> <br>
                        We can save our file whereever we want on the C: drive, and read it from there. <br>
                        Also, we do not need to include the <b>System.IO.File namespace</b> as we did in the first approach. <br>
                        I shall not include it. But you may include it if you want to. <br>
                        So, we shall copy the file: <b>Module7Notes.txt</b> from where we saved it and save it in a new location. <br>
                        The new location is: <b>C:\Users\chukw\OneDrive\Documents\C#\Files and Streams\Module7Notes.txt</b> <br>
                        As you can see: I saved it in the Files and Streams folder of the C# folder of the Documents File and folder of the
                        OneDrive on my computer. <br>
                        Yes, I saved it in the cloud. <br>
                        Yes, I can make changes to it (append some texts to it) in the cloud. <br>
                        The easy way to figure out the path/location of your file is: Right-click the file; click the Properties icon; and
                        copy the Location. You do want to make sure you include the file name to the path in the program. <br>
                        When using the <b>ReadAllText method of the System.IO.File class</b>, it is important to enclose the path/location
                        of the file enclosed in double quotes, and include the <b>at</b> symbol, <b>@</b> before it. <br>
                        Please review the screenshot and the comments. <br><br>

                    <h4>Example $3$: Write to a new text file using the StreamWriter class, then Read from that file using the ReadAllLines
                        method of the System.IO.File class</h4>
                    <p>
                        We shall use the <b>StreamWriter</b> class to create a new text file and write to it. <br><br>
                        <i>
                            <b>Student:</b> But, why not just write to the one we created already? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Good question. <br>
                            This is because once we write to it, it will erase everything we had already. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> What if we wanted to add more notes to the file we just read? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> You are asking good questions. <br>
                            We shall <b>append</b> notes to it. <br>
                            Rather than write to it, we shall append to it. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Why did you decide to use the <b>ReadAllLines method</b> this time? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> When we use the <b>StreamWriter class</b> to write to a new file, that file is saved in the
                            <b>Documents</b> folder of the <b>Windows (C:) drive</b> by default. <br>
                            <i>C:\Users\chukw\Documents\...the...new...file</i> <br>
                            Because of the location where it is saved, we cannot use the <b>StreamReader class</b> to read it. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> I am not talking about the <b>StreamReader class</b> though. <br>
                            I am asking why you want to use <b>ReadAllLines method</b> rather than the one we used earlier: the
                            <b>ReadAllText method</b> <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> The <b>StreamWriter class</b> uses a <b>foreach loop</b> to write contents to a file because we
                            use an array to write the contents. <br>
                            The <b>ReadAllLines method</b> reads each line of a file into an array. Each element of the array is one line of
                            the file. <br>
                            Because of that, we also use a <b>foreach loop</b> to read each line of the file. <br>
                            In that regard, I prefer using the <b>ReadAllLines method</b> to read whatever I write with the <b>StreamWriter
                                class</b> becuase they both use an array and a foreach loop. <br>
                            Notice that the <b>ReadAllText</b> method did not use an array.
                        </i> <br><br>
                        Let us use another topic from another field. <br>
                        We shall include the <b>System.IO.File namespace</b> because we want to use the <b>StreamWriter class</b> directly.
                        <br>
                        This is one of the acronyms I learned in Elementary School: Characteristics of Living Things: MRNIGER. <br>
                        Please review the screenshots and the comments. <br><br>
                    
                        <img src="images/AModule6CSharp/write-file-read-file-1-1.png" alt="Write File and Read File 1-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule6CSharp/write-file-read-file-1-2.png" alt="Write File and Read File 1-2"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule6CSharp/write-file-read-file-1-3.png" alt="Write File and Read File 1-3"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule6CSharp/write-file-read-file-1-4.png" alt="Write File and Read File 1-4"> <br><br>
                    
                        <b>NOTE:</b> <br>
                        Whenever you write to a file, it is very important you close the file before reading it. <br>
                        <i>So, did we close the file before reading it?</i> <br>
                        As seen from the code: the <b>StreamWriter</b> object, <b>writeFile</b> is declared and instantiated in the
                        <b>using</b> statement. <br>
                        Because of this, the <b>Dispose method</b> is invoked. <br>
                        The <b>Dispose method</b> automatically flushes and closes the stream. <br>
                        Hence, the file was <i>actually</i> closed before we read it. <br><br>
                    
                        <i>
                            <b>Student:</b> Mr. C, you are aware you did not complete the listing. <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Yes Sir. I am aware. <br>
                            Guess I did it intentionally. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Why? <br>
                            To show the <b>Append</b> method? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> To show the <b>Append</b> operation.
                        </i>
                    </p>
                    
                    <h4>Example $4$: Append to an existing text file using the StreamWriter class, then Read from that file using the
                        ReadAllLines method of the System.IO.File class
                    </h4>
                    <p>
                        We have already created the <b>LivingThings.txt</b> file. <br>
                        However, what we listed in that file is incomplete. <br>
                        We need to complete it. <br>
                        So, we need to <b>append</b> some texts to it. <br>
                        If you are appending each characteristic to a new line, use the <b>WriteLine</b> method for each characteristic <br>
                        If you are appending some texts to a characteristic on the same line, use the <b>Write</b> method. <br>
                        After appending the texts, we shall read the entire file. <br>
                        Please review the screenshots and the comments. <br><br>
                    
                        <img src="images/AModule6CSharp/append-file-read-file-1-1.png" alt="Append File and Read File 1-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule6CSharp/append-file-read-file-1-2.png" alt="Append File and Read File 1-2"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule6CSharp/append-file-read-file-1-3.png" alt="Append File and Read File 1-3"> <br><br>
                    
                        <b>NOTE:</b> <br>
                        Whenever you write to a file, it is very important you close the file before reading it. <br>
                        Appending to a file is <i>actually</i> writing to an existing file. <br>
                        <i>So, did we close the file before reading it?</i> <br>
                        As seen from the code: the <b>StreamWriter</b> object, <b>writeFile</b> is declared and instantiated in the
                        <b>using</b> statement. <br>
                        Because of this, the <b>Dispose method</b> is invoked. <br>
                        The <b>Dispose method</b> automatically flushes and closes the stream. <br>
                        Hence, the file was <i>actually</i> closed before we read it.
                    </p>
                    
                    <h4>Example $5$: Write to a new text file using the File class, Append to that file using the File Class, then Read from
                        that file using the ReadAllText method of the System.IO.File class</h4>
                    <p>
                        Besides the <b>StreamWriter class</b> for writing and appending to files, we can also the <b>WriteAllText method</b>
                        and the <b>AppendAllText method</b> of the <b>File class</b> <br>
                        The <b>WriteAllText method</b> creates, writes, and closes the file automatically. <br>
                        The <b>AppendAllText method</b> opens, writes, and closes the file automatically. <br>
                        So, you do not need to call the Flush() or Close() method. <br><br>
                        Let us use a quote from Martin Luther King Jr. <br>
                        We shall write the incomplete quote. <br>
                        Then, append some text to complete it. <br>
                        Then, read the quote. <br>
                        <i>Do not forget the escape sequence for including double quotes</i> <br><br>
                    
                        <img src="images/AModule6CSharp/write-file-append-file-read-file-1-1.png" alt="Write File, Append File and Read File 1-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule6CSharp/write-file-append-file-read-file-1-2.png" alt="Write File, Append File and Read File 1-2"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule6CSharp/write-file-append-file-read-file-1-3.png" alt="Write File, Append File and Read File 1-3"> <br><br>
                    
                        <i>
                            <b>Student:</b> If we want to append more lines rather than just text, we shall use the <b>AppendAllLines
                                method</b> and the <b>ReadAllLines method</b>? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> That is correct! <br>
                            Let us do a case where we use the <b>AppendAllLines method</b> <br>
                            We are doing these various examples with several approaches so you can choose which one you prefer.
                        </i>
                    </p>

                    <h4>Example $6$: Write to a new text file, Append to that file, then Read from that file using the System.IO.File class
                        methods</h4>
                    <p>
                        The inclusion of the <b>System.IO.File namespace</b> is not required for this approach because the approach I want
                        to use here would allow me to save the file on OneDrive (my preference). You can save it whereever you wish. <br>
                        So, I shall not be including that namespace. <br>
                        But, you may choose to include it if you wish. It does not matter. <br>
                        We shall use the: <b>WriteAllLines method</b>, <b>AppendAllLines method</b>, and the <b>ReadAllLines method</b> in
                        this approach. <br><br>
                    
                        <i>
                            <b>Student:</b> Which example will you use this time? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Guess what I'm thinking? <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> What? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> About a mnemonic we learned in Junior Secondary School in Nigeria <br>
                            Junior Secondary School in Nigeria is equivalent to Middle School in the United States. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> What is the mnemonic? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> The mnemonic that helped us to write the first twenty elements of the Periodic Table? <br>
                            I even made a YouTube video about it. <br>
                            <b>Student:</b> Interesting. <br>
                            Where is the video? <br>
                            Can you write the elements off hand? <br>
                            <b>Teacher:</b> Sure &#128522; <br>
                            The link is: <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=XgUEwvS4NGM" target="_blank">1st 20 Elements of the Periodic Table</a> <br>
                            Let us write the first the mnemonic, the element, and the symbol for the first ten elements. <br>
                            Then, append the same for the last ten elements. <br>
                            Then, read the file. <br>
                            We shall name the file: <b>Elements.txt</b> and save it in this location:
                            <b>C:\Users\chukw\OneDrive\Documents\C#\Files and Streams\Elements.txt</b> <br>
                            Let's do it.
                        </i> <br><br>
                    
                        <img src="images/AModule6CSharp/write-file-append-file-read-file-2-1.png" alt="Write File, Append File and Read File 2-1"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule6CSharp/write-file-append-file-read-file-2-2.png" alt="Write File, Append File and Read File 2-2"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule6CSharp/write-file-append-file-read-file-2-3.png" alt="Write File, Append File and Read File 2-3"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule6CSharp/write-file-append-file-read-file-2-4.png" alt="Write File, Append File and Read File 2-4"> <br>
                        <img src="images/AModule6CSharp/write-file-append-file-read-file-2-5.png" alt="Write File, Append File and Read File 2-5"> <br><br>
                    
                        The <b>WriteAllLines method</b> creates, writes, and closes the file automatically. <br>
                        The <b>AppendAllLines method</b> opens, writes, and closes the file automatically. <br>
                        So, you do not need to call the Flush() or Close() method. <br><br>
                    
                        <i>Which of these approaches do you prefer?</i>
                    </p>
                </div>

                            It is important you communicate with me for approval prior to doing this project.
                        </mark> <br>
                        Because the course deals with <u>only</u> <b>desktop applications</b> and <b>console applications</b>, 
                        (rather than web applications and mobile applications), you <b>may not do any project that requires a web server</b>
                        such as contact forms and registration forms among others. <br><br>
                    
                        The final project is designed to assess problem-solving skills using the knowledge you have acquired in the 
                        course. <br>
                        In that regard, solving at least a real-world problem using the knowledge gained in the course is expected. <br><br>

                        (1.) Think about any of these companies: our Institution: Kent State University; your local campus; your work place; public and 
                        private schools (excluding home schools); organizations, firms, and businesses 
                        (excluding controversial firms and the like). <br>
                        Any company you choose should have a verifiable website. <br>
                        The direct link of the company's website is required. <br><br>

                        (2.) Identify a problem on the website that you want to solve. <br>
                        The problem must be solved by programming using the knowledge acquired from any of the course 
                        topics/concepts/description/learning objectives. <br>
                        <i>Think about your Midterm Project. You solved a problem: calculating bills, federal taxes, etc.</i> <br><br>

                        (3.) Develop the computer program to solve the problem. <br>
                        Test the program to ensure that it works. <br>
                        Write detailed comments as applicable. <br><br>

                        (4.) Prepare a Documentation Manual/Reflection for the project. <br>
                        Please cite your sources accordingly. <br>
                        What is the company? <br>
                        What is their website? <br>
                        What problem did you identify? <br>
                        Did you solve the entire problem or part of the problem? <br>
                        What <b>course topics/concepts/description/learning objectives</b> did you <b>apply</b> in solving the problem? <br>
                        How did you solve the problem? <br>
                        Did you encounter any issues while trying to solve the problem? <br>
                        Are there any limitations to the project? <br>
                        Are there any recommendations to the company? <br><br>
                        
                        <b>Deliverables:</b> <br>
                        (1.) Program (all files) <br>
                        (2.) Clear screenshots of your program (code and output) <br>
                        (3.) Project Reflection/Documentation Manual <br><br>
                        Please submit all these information as a zipped folder (.zip only) in the <b>Final Project</b> folder of the Blackboard course.
                    </p>
